#5063. Google AI agents audit Signal’s code and one major flaw stays hidden

Google DeepMind's AI agents generated 6.5 million lines of machine-checked proofs verifying security properties in Signal's code.

Google DeepMind researchers say their AI agents produced mathematical proofs linking specific security guarantees to a compiled build of Signal’s encryption library, and they put the resulting evidence online for others to check.

The Nov. 10 AlphaProof Code report describes about 6.5 million lines of machine-checked Lean proof code. The result concerns a particular build of libsignal, the Signal protocol’s implementation, under explicit assumptions about cryptography and the environment in which the code runs.

The authors say five people worked on the proofs over four weeks. Humans directed the work, selected reference models and reviewed the specifications that defined what the agents had to prove.

The target was an AArch64 Linux executable using libsignal-protocol v0.105.4 and SPQR v1.6.0 inside a small command-line harness. The theorems cover session initialization, sending a message, and receiving one. Key generation falls outside that verified scope.

The Google report connects the compiled executable to specified properties of PQXDH and Triple Ratchet one-to-one messaging, including forward secrecy, authentication, and post-compromise security.

These address whether earlier messages stay secret after keys are stolen, whether senders are authentic, and whether a session can regain secrecy after a compromise.

Its two proof models provide different assurances: the symbolic analysis relies on idealized cryptographic operations and collision-free executions, while the computational analysis bounds the success of classical attackers under stated cryptographic assumptions.

DeepMind’s reported process connects human-selected requirements and one executable to a machine-checked proof, with trusted assumptions still defining the result.

A checked proof establishes the stated guarantee for the specified build and model.

Author-reported proofs for a specific AArch64 Linux harness. Specifications, execution model, routine contracts and cryptographic assumptions remain trusted. No direct verification of phone apps, key generation or groups; no independent rebuild performed here.

Source-bound schematic of the reported proof workflow and trust boundary; connectors show logical dependence, not elapsed time or measured security strength.

Author-reported proofs for a specific AArch64 Linux harness. Specifications, execution model, routine contracts and cryptographic assumptions remain trusted. No direct verification of phone apps, key generation or groups; no independent rebuild performed here.

The symbolic proof establishes handshake secrecy under specified later breaks of Diffie-Hellman, but the computational proof does not cover quantum attackers. Group messaging is outside the report’s scope, and the computational model excludes multi-device state. Its bounds also stop providing a useful guarantee at sufficiently large workloads.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The results still require trust in Lean’s checking kernel, the specifications reviewed by the researchers, the AArch64 Linux model, and contracts describing selected routines.

The project’s Nov. 11 update, merged on GitHub, says ten more library routines were verified, cutting trusted routine hooks from 37 to 27. Those hooks replace execution of selected routines with assumed descriptions of their behavior.

The update retains libsignal v0.105.4 but changes the pinned executable. The newer build verifies the machine code for those ten routines instead of relying on their modeled contracts.

The project repository provides public instructions to rebuild the pinned binary and check its proofs. A Linux-only comparator checks that the theorems and their dependent definitions match trusted statements, then replays proofs in a fresh Lean kernel.

The authors also report reintroducing two previously fixed libsignal bugs and proving that the modified binaries violate their symbolic security theorem. That tests whether the specification rejects those known regressions.

The public package gives other researchers a concrete way to check the claimed connection between security requirements and executable code.

#5062. Stellar Floats XLM Rewards Pool After 1,000% Payments Jump

Home » Trendsetter News » Stellar Floats XLM Rewards Pool After 1,000% Payments Jump

Stellar is exploring an XLM rewards pool funded by network fees as monthly payment volume rises tenfold.

Stellar is considering a proposal to reward XLM holders who lock tokens and contribute utility to the network. Crypto market commentator All In Crypto highlights the idea in a video reviewing Stellar’s third-quarter webinar, asking whether rising network activity can translate into stronger demand for XLM.

The proposal would put network-generated fees into an XLM rewards pool. But Dixon, speaking during the webinar, stresses that it remains an early concept open to feedback, not an approved change or a promised payout.

“I’m not sure this is the right proposal,” she says, urging participants to read the accompanying paper and explore its interactive dashboard before commenting. Her central argument is that “utility should drive XLM.”

The webinar reports that monthly payment volume on Stellar rose from roughly $1.8 billion in January to $18.5 billion in October. October’s figure was 89% higher than September’s, following eight consecutive months of growth.

Another $7.5 billion reportedly moved during November’s first 10 days. Those figures describe money moving across the network, however, not purchases of XLM or returns accruing to token holders.

All In Crypto is openly bullish but acknowledges uncertainty over the token’s future value. That distinction matters: greater use of Stellar’s infrastructure does not, by itself, establish how much additional XLM users need to hold.

The webinar also describes a U.S. Bank pilot in which the bank issued a dollar-backed stablecoin called USBDC and used it to settle a cross-border payment between its North American and European entities. According to the presentation, minting, redemption, freezing and claw-back controls operated at the protocol level, without custom contract logic.

Stellar’s Project Throughput offers another glimpse of its ambitions. The webinar cites early benchmarks of roughly 1.2 million payments per second on one machine and about seven million across eight machines.

Those are technology-preview results, not reported production-network throughput. The project is available on Futurenet with open-source code for developers to test.

Now, the unresolved question is less whether Stellar can process more activity than how that activity benefits XLM.

The rewards proposal could create another connection between network use and token ownership, but its design, eligibility requirements and eventual adoption remain unsettled.

#5060. Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has thrown his weight behind a proposal he says could make the network quantum-safe much faster than expected.

"Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull," Buterin tweeted on Monday, in a lengthy explainer on how Ethereum could handle the much larger signatures and proofs that quantum-resistant cryptography requires.

Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull. So let me explain some of these buzzwords in more detail.

At the core, 8288 is about hyper-scaling data and execution for any computation that needs to get performed on a piece of data…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 16, 2026

In short, leanPCS is a proposed way for Ethereum to bundle many large, quantum-safe signatures and proofs into a single compact proof, so that the full data never has to be stored onchain. It is built on polynomial commitment schemes, or PCS, the core mathematical building block of STARK proofs.

Quantum computers are expected to eventually break the elliptic curve cryptography that secures Ethereum and Bitcoin accounts today. The quantum-safe replacements are far bulkier: Buterin said signatures would grow from 65 bytes to between 1 and 6 kilobytes, while zero-knowledge proofs would balloon from a few hundred bytes to around 128 kilobytes. "This is unsustainable to put on chain," he wrote.

Buterin's answer is EIP-8288, a draft proposal he co-authored with developer Thomas Coratger in June, which would aggregate those signatures and proofs before they ever reach a block.

Under the design, users publish their signatures or proofs, and network nodes repeatedly bundle everything they have seen into a single STARK, a type of zero-knowledge proof, that attests the underlying data is valid. These aggregate proofs are themselves combined until a single "master proof" verifies everything in a block. Only short claims, rather than the full signatures or proofs, would need to be stored onchain.

He laid out three ways to build it, depending on what users submit. The simplest, leanSPHINCS, would support a single type of quantum-safe, hash-based signature, costing users only a small amount of gas per signature, but it couldn't do anything else.

Buterin's preferred option, leanPCS, offers a third route. It would offer the same generality as leanSTARK but "could potentially be implemented very quickly," he said, because far fewer design choices are needed, and leanSTARK could then be added later.

"So there is a route to getting 'the cryptographic proof singularity' much faster than any of us expected," Buterin wrote, "and that route runs through 8288 with leanPCS."

Buterin linked to SPHINCS-, a family of hash-based signature schemes optimized for Ethereum, which was published on the Ethereum Research forum in June with input from Buterin and Ethereum Foundation cryptographers. The post shows that a standards-aligned quantum-safe signature can already be verified on Ethereum for about 150,000 gas, without any protocol change, compared with about 3,000 gas for an ECDSA signature today, according to an earlier Buterin estimate.

Under protocol-level aggregation, a future "leanSPHINCS" verification would cost about 3,000 gas, according to the post.

#5059. Anthropic’s Claude builds custom gadget blueprints, but consistency stays hidden

Anthropic's Claude designed circuit boards, enclosures and firmware for three working gadgets assembled and tested by engineers.

Anthropic says Claude turned short gadget briefs into designs for three working prototypes, coordinating circuit boards, enclosures, and firmware that human engineers then assembled and tested.

In a Nov. 9 research post, Anthropic described a speakerphone, a voice-assistant hub, and a handheld virtual pet designed by Claude Fable 5.2, while Diode Computers built and tested the

For the speakerphone, Anthropic said Claude produced specifications and schematics, placed components on a printed circuit board, routed the copper connections, designed a 3D-printable case, wrote the device's software, and prepared a parts list. The other two devices followed a similar process.

Anthropic reports three working gadgets from linked electronics, enclosure and firmware designs. Diode engineers performed the physical work.

The demonstration joins several design disciplines, while people build and test hardware and commercial readiness remains unresolved.

Company and commercial-partner account. Three working examples do not establish a repeated-attempt success rate. Electrical validation, antenna tuning, thermal testing and certification remain undone; devices were not designed for mass production.

Qualitative handoff diagram from Anthropic's account. Three branches identify design disciplines, then converge into human construction and testing. Device silhouettes are schematic illustrations, not engineering drawings; connector lengths encode no time or performance.

Company and commercial-partner account. Three working examples do not establish a repeated-attempt success rate. Electrical validation, antenna tuning, thermal testing and certification remain undone; devices were not designed for mass production.

Diode supplied occasional follow-up directives, and an engineer asked whether the virtual pet would operate without a battery. Claude was not connected to test equipment or the machines that make and assemble boards, so people performed the physical construction and testing.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

According to Anthropic, Claude checked whether the speakerphone's board would fit inside its case and found that the mute switch would collide with a wall during insertion. It added an assembly step that tilted the board into place.

For the voice-assistant hub, Claude changed the enclosure's shape to shorten microphone ports. It also wrote speakerphone firmware that dimmed the LEDs as audio became louder, keeping the device within the board's designed electrical-current limit, Anthropic said.

Anthropic said Diode manufactured all three boards directly from Claude's design files, without exchanging manufacturing adjustments, and that the prototypes worked without circuitry fixes.

Anthropic said they had not undergone electrical validation, antenna tuning, thermal testing or certification and were not designed for mass production. A functioning prototype still needs substantial engineering work before it becomes a commercially ready product.

The disclosure also provides no overall success rate across repeated end-to-end design attempts. Its three working examples show what the collaborators say they achieved, while leaving unanswered how consistently the process could deliver another usable device.

Anthropic's planned next steps include denser, faster, and more complex boards, simulation checks, and learning from measurements of finished devices. For engineers and hardware builders, that would extend the work from producing a design toward checking how it behaves once built.

#5055. XRP’s 10-Year RSI Low Fuels Debate Over a Bottom

Home » Trendsetter News » XRP’s 10-Year RSI Low Fuels Debate Over a Bottom

Analysts see possible recovery signals, but extreme oversold conditions don’t confirm a reversal: further deficit remains possible.

XRP’s daily relative strength index has fallen to its lowest reading since 2016, according to chart analysis cited by Matt, the cryptocurrency YouTuber presenting the new Moon Lambo video episode.

His central argument: extreme selling pressure may signal an opportunity, even as XRP price trades below $1 and investors remain deeply fearful.

In the recording, dated November 17, 2026, Matt puts XRP just below $0.96 and Bitcoin at $64,571. He also cites a Crypto Fear and Greed Index reading of 12 out of 100, firmly in “extreme fear” territory. Those figures are presented in the video rather than independently verified market data.

The most striking figure comes from Crypto Insight UK: an XRP daily RSI of 16.17, described as its lowest in a decade. Matt acknowledges the limitation directly: “Just because we’re oversold doesn’t mean we can’t go lower.”

The weekly chart offers a less straightforward signal. Citing analyst D Greg Crypto, Matt describes XRP’s weekly RSI falling below 20, recovering that level and then quickly testing it again—an unusually compressed sequence compared with previous recoveries.

Under that analysis, holding above 20 could support a potential double-bottom momentum structure. A sustained break below it would instead leave room for further capitulation. The distinction matters: an extreme indicator reading is not the same thing as a confirmed price bottom.

Matt also highlights Charting Guy’s view that XRP resembles a previous cycle’s bottoming process, with a weekly bullish divergence and a possible re-accumulation pattern. That analyst maintains a minimum xrp price target of $4.36, not a forecast for the eventual peak.

The timing is considerably less certain. Charting Guy suggests possible altcoin seasons in summer 2027 and late 2028, while Matt argues that Bitcoin’s direction will remain important for XRP and the wider market.

Matt’s own response is to keep holding rather than trade the volatility. He identifies himself as an enthusiast without a financial background, not a financial adviser.

For now, the consequential question is whether exhausted selling develops into sustained buying. Moon Lambo presents several arguments for recovery, but its own weekly-RSI discussion leaves a clear risk unresolved: XRP price could remain under pressure before any broader market rebound takes hold.

#5054. Satoshi-Era Bitcoin Holder Wakes Up After 15 Years, Refuting $250 Billion ‘Lost’ Claim

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

A Bitcoin address that had not moved coins since February 2011 transferred 75 BTC on Nov. 17, according to Galaxy Research. At the current price of $64,935 per BTC, the coins are worth around $4.87 million.

The address is listed as a defendant in a $250 billion lawsuit in New York Supreme Court, in which the plaintiffs are trying to have early Bitcoin wallets declared "lost." Galaxy Research said that several other addresses from the same list have also moved coins in 2026.

According to Galaxy Research analysts, early on Nov. 17, in block 976,209, the address 1ALpEWtXApR19hLXvcG8NfkPyGsUMhFPMS sent coins for the first time. It was first funded on Feb. 8, 2011. At that time, Bitcoin was trading at about $0.90. After 15.8 years, the coins went to an unknown address with a 399-satoshi fee, or about 26 cents, for a paper gain of more than 7,000,000%.

Onchain databases list the wallet as Noah Doe #3409 (Salomon Client Dusted). That label is how it ended up among the defendants in the high-profile case ABC Company, XYZ Company, and Noah Doe v. John Does 1–39,069.

According to the lawsuit, an anonymous plaintiff using the pseudonym Noah Doe and two companies want the rights to 39,069 Bitcoin wallets that have not moved coins for more than five years. The plaintiffs said that these wallets hold about 3.8 million BTC, and at the current price, the coins are worth around $250 billion.

The plaintiffs filed the lawsuit under New York's lost-and-found law, Article 7-B of the Personal Property Law. Under the law, a person who finds lost property has to give it to the police, and the property goes to the finder if the owner does not claim it in time. In the lawsuit, the plaintiffs valued each address at less than $10, and they argued that the waiting period for such items was one year.

They "found" dormant addresses with an algorithm, saved them onto USB flash drives and took them to the New York Police Department (NYPD). Then they served the owners on the blockchain, through microtransactions with embedded messages (OP_RETURN). The practice is known as "dusting," and the campaign is tied to Salomon Brothers Strategic Advisors. Once the waiting period ran out, the plaintiffs asked the court to recognize the coins as theirs.

The case has been put on hold several times over motions to dismiss and amicus curiae briefs from outside experts. At a hearing on Sept. 8, the Bitcoin Policy Institute asked to intervene as a defendant, and The Digital Chamber asked to join the case as amicus curiae. Galaxy Research described it as a case "in which anonymous plaintiffs are seeking to claim legal title over Satoshi's coins."

Galaxy Research's Will Owens, who attended the hearing, said that The Digital Chamber's arguments were "well pleaded" and that "the judge sounded sympathetic to them."

Critics of the lawsuit argue that Article 7-B was written for things you can pick up and hand over for safekeeping. It wasn't written for blockchain records when the "finder" doesn't hold the private keys.

In September, Galaxy head of research Alex Thorn also said that the Lost and Found Property Law includes "protection from 'Noah Doe-style' dormancy attacks on legal ownership."

#5052. XRP volume surges 99% to $62M, yet small sellers lose out

XRP Ledger trading volume surged 100% as improved RLUSD order-book depth reduced modeled costs for large XRP sales.

XRP-paired trading on its native ledger nearly doubled over Nov. 9-15, but a separate comparison of the direct market for Ripple USD (RLUSD) at two dated ledgers showed a smaller execution penalty for a 100,000-XRP sale.

DefiLlama's daily data put completed Nov. 9-15 trading at $62.6 million, up 99.9% from $31.3 million during Nov. 2-8.

The measure combines XRP-paired automated market maker swaps and order-book trades. Native XRP participates directly, but repeated turnover can recycle existing inventory without establishing fresh net buying.

NOSHASHI's ledger data showed the XRP/RLUSD automated market maker holding 2.165 million RLUSD at Nov. 9's close and 1.861 million on Nov. 16, a 14% decline. Native XRP in the same pool slipped from 1.972 million to 1.959 million.

Over that interval, sampled funded RLUSD offers available to token sellers increased from about 19,392 to 111,951 RLUSD, roughly 5.77 times the earlier amount. Funded amounts measure what the offer owners could deliver and may be below the advertised size.

NOSHASHI's measurement requests at most 100 offers per side and counts funded depth within 10% of each snapshot's reference midpoint. Its ask side is the RLUSD inventory an XRP seller receives, and the figures exclude AMM reserves, do not measure all XRP liquidity, and do not imply that every sampled offer sits near the best price.

An idealized static model combined the Nov. 9 raw offers and pool state with their Nov. 16 equivalents. For each snapshot, it allocated XRP between funded offers and the constant-product pool according to which supplied more RLUSD for the next XRP sold.

The pool calculation includes its ordinary trading fee, 0.308% at the earlier ledger and 0.315% at the later one. XRPL can combine offers and AMMs in an actual transaction, but this calculation is not account-specific pathfinding or a completed fill.

The benchmark is the midpoint of the sampled book's tightest uncrossed bid and ask quotes, equivalent to about 1.098 RLUSD per XRP on Nov. 9 and 0.951 on Nov. 16.

In CryptoSlate's calculations, the shortfall for 100,000 XRP narrowed from 3.57% to 0.48% against each snapshot's own reference midpoint.

This compares modeled proceeds with the token amount implied by that reference price and includes spread, modeled AMM fees, and price impact. The lower absolute proceeds on Nov. 16 reflect XRP's lower price in RLUSD, despite the smaller relative penalty.

The smaller trades did not share that improvement: the 1,000-XRP shortfall widened from 0.05% to 0.11%, while at 10,000 XRP, it widened from 0.05% to 0.19%.

In the later snapshot, the larger modeled sale relied more heavily on funded offers. On Nov. 9, only about 17,701 XRP went to the sampled book, leaving 82,299 XRP for the pool. On Nov. 16, about 88,471 XRP went to offers and 11,529 XRP to the pool.

Swaps and liquidity-provider deposits or withdrawals can all change pool balances. A dollar-valued total value locked decline can also reflect repricing: DefiLlama's pool methodology excludes order-book offers and can double a pool's sole priced side, so its valuation buckets cannot establish literal token holdings or net deposits.

RLUSD proceeds also differ from dollars in a bank account. Ripple's user terms require Customer status under its customer agreement for direct issuer redemption, subject to applicable conditions.

The next useful evidence is whether funded offers persist and repeatable estimates remain favorable at the intended sale size.

This sample supports the conclusion that the larger direct RLUSD exit became less costly in the model even as the pool's RLUSD reserve shrank.

#5050. German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

Germany's parliament has approved sweeping new powers for the country's customs agency, allowing it to seize assets, including crypto, whose lawful origin is in doubt, even when no criminal investigation is underway.

The Bundestag passed the Customs Financial Justice Act, or Zollfinanzgerechtigkeitsgesetz, on Thursday, November 12, the German government said. Under the law, customs can more easily seize assets where there are "justified doubts" about their lawful origin, an instrument the government describes as a "suspicious wealth order." Customs will also be able to better monitor digital cross-border payment routes and secure crypto assets.

"With this, we are sending a clear signal to criminals: we are getting tougher," German Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil said, according to the government.

The powers apply to "significant" assets, which typically means those worth more than €100,000, according to the government's bill. Because owners of such assets are usually able to explain where they came from, while the state struggles to prove an illegal origin, the government argued that it is reasonable to ask owners to disclose that information. "Almost every law-abiding citizen will be able to explain where these assets came from," the bill's explanatory notes say, as summarized by the Bundestag's news service.

Klingbeil has said customs could hold assets for up to 180 days without a conviction, during which owners would have to show they were obtained legally. "The Porsche and the Rolex are then gone for now," he said, according to the Berliner Zeitung. Any permanent confiscation would require a court ruling. Following an amendment by the governing coalition, that decision will be made by a panel of finance court judges rather than a single judge.

The law also reorganizes Germany's customs administration, strengthens its Financial Intelligence Unit and gives customs a legal basis to use technologies such as artificial intelligence. It forms part of an action plan against organized crime that the government adopted on February 25, 2026.

Germany has seized large amounts of crypto before, and has been quick to sell it. In 2024, authorities in the state of Saxony began selling the nearly 50,000 BTC they had seized from operators of the movie piracy website Movie2K, moving hundreds of millions of dollars' worth at a time as the sell-off continued. Blockchain analytics firm Arkham later said authorities had failed to seize a further 45,000 BTC linked to the site. The new law would give customs a way to seize crypto of unclear origin without first having to tie it to a specific crime.

German authorities have also gone after laundering infrastructure. Last year, they joined Swiss police in shutting down Cryptomixer, a mixing service that had laundered more than $1.4 billion in Bitcoin since 2016. Separately, the finance ministry has drafted a 25% tax on crypto gains from 2027.

The Bundestag's finance committee backed the bill on Wednesday with votes from the governing CDU/CSU and SPD, as well as the far-right AfD. The Left Party voted against, and the Greens abstained.

The Greens said Germany's money laundering problem is estimated at €100 billion, and that the government's expectation of only €308 million in additional revenue showed the gap between its ambitions and the law. The Left Party said the confiscation process was "built for the cash courier, not the banker."

The law still needs to pass the Bundesrat, the upper house of parliament that represents Germany's states.

#5049. Ripple-Backed Evernorth Takes Massive $505 Million XRP Hit

Ripple-backed XRP treasury company Evernorth Holdings has disclosed a significant decrease in the book value of its crypto asset holdings, with total impairment charges amounting to around $505 million before going public on Nasdaq.

Per an SEC filing on Nov. 13, the 346.3 million XRP tokens that Evernorth Holdings owned had a book value of just $341.9 million as of Sept. 30, 2026.

This is compared to the firm's initial accounting of the crypto assets at roughly $846.6 million, with an average acquisition or contribution price of $2.44 per XRP. This represents a nearly 60% decline in book value.

As per the latest filing, Evernorth recorded around $271 million in XRP impairment charges in the first nine months of 2026. This is in addition to the $233.7 million in impairment charges recorded in 2025.

Interestingly, only $6.9 million of the recent impairment charges occurred in the third quarter.

This also explains why Evernorth's XRP assets had a book value so far below their market value at the end of September.

Evernorth calculates impairments based on the lowest observable intraday price of XRP for each batch of tokens acquired. Once the assets are written down, the company is unable to reverse the previous losses if the price rises.

For reference, on Sept. 30, XRP closed the day trading at around $1.49 on Coinbase. At that price, Evernorth's 346.3 million XRP tokens would have been worth roughly $516 million, much higher than the disclosed book value of $341.9 million.

The recent disclosure comes around a month after Evernorth completed its business combination with Armada Acquisition Corp. II on Oct. 9. It started trading on Nasdaq under the symbol XRPN on Oct. 12.

Following the business combination, Evernorth held around 473 million XRP tokens and had also raised gross cash proceeds of around $300 million.

Its investors include Ripple, SBI Group, Arrington Capital, Pantera Capital, Kraken and GSR.

On Oct. 14, Evernorth announced collaborations with Ripple, Kraken, Coinbase and BitGo to implement institutional investment strategies starting in Q4 2026.

Meanwhile, its latest SEC filing did not include details on the amount of XRP deployed to generate yield or the returns generated from it.

#5047. A $25 million MEV ruling exposes a dangerous blind spot in Wall Street’s tokenization push

An Ethereum exploit case has brought renewed attention to transaction-ordering risks as the SEC allows tokenized stock venues to set their own safeguards.

A federal judge on Nov. 16 acquitted Anton and James Peraire-Bueno in a $25 million Ethereum maximum extractable value (MEV) exploit case because prosecutors had failed to prove a material misrepresentation.

The decision leaves a separate question for tokenized US stock markets about what protects investors when transaction ordering works against them.

The SEC had already opened a conditional route for those markets in September on its framework, which preserves applicable securities antifraud and manipulation laws, but requires venues to disclose their policies for MEV even when they have none.

In her 46-page opinion, US District Judge Jessica Clarke found insufficient evidence to sustain wire fraud and the related wire-fraud and money-laundering conspiracy charges. The missing element was a material misrepresentation in the brothers' Omakase operation.

The opinion describes a 2023 operation that used lure transactions to attract sandwich-trading bots and exploited a relay-code vulnerability, producing about $25 million in 12 seconds. The targeted bots themselves sought to profit by placing transactions around someone else's trade, a practice that can leave that trader with a worse execution price.

The case was more specific than a simple claim that someone profited from transaction ordering, since prosecutors had to establish the charged fraud.

Clarke found that the trial evidence did not establish a material misrepresentation in the Omakase strategy, and frustrating MEV-Boost's intended design could not supply that missing element.

The court also distinguished precedents involving spoofing, where orders create a misleading impression before being canceled. Here, the lure transactions executed and also were not placed on a regulated market with rules prohibiting the conduct relied upon in those precedents.

That reasoning does not establish a universal requirement that wire fraud occur on a regulated market, nor does it determine whether all forms of MEV extraction are lawful.

In a March 30 letter, the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association (SIFMA) argued that exposing pending trades to sandwiching and front-running can conflict with securities-market expectations. It called for technical protections or fair-sequencing rules when automated market makers handle securities.

An automated market maker (AMM) lets participants trade against assets committed to a pool using software-defined pricing. The ordering of those trades can affect who receives a favorable price and who captures the resulting profit.

Keeping an investor's pending instruction private and deciding who processes it first are economically significant choices.

The SEC's Sept. 17 order created temporary, conditional relief from the definition of an exchange for qualifying tokenized National Market System stock venues using permissioned AMM pools. Certain liquidity providers trading proprietary capital received separate relief from the dealer definition until Sept. 17, 2031.

The order retains applicable securities antifraud and antimanipulation provisions, including Exchange Act Section 10(b) and Rule 10b-5. Its conditions include permissioned access, public notices, records, and specified trading stoppages.

A venue must describe policies and procedures designed to address it, or state that it has none. It must disclose known material risks, explain any mitigation and compensation arrangements, and describe trading-abuse monitoring or acknowledge its absence.

A venue's published account of its protections can be detailed while leaving investors exposed to risks the operator expressly acknowledges.

The SEC's public list, reviewed Nov. 16, contains four notice filers. Their submission dates range from TSV LLC's Sept. 23 notice to Formation Securities' Nov. 9 filing.

TSV LLC's current notice says Canton's architecture generally avoids broadcasting readable pending transaction contents to unrelated participants, aiming to reduce opportunities for conventional public-mempool front-running and sandwich attacks.

The operator still acknowledges risks of information leakage, sequencing, compromised infrastructure, and collusion.

OKXICE's Nov. 5 notice disclosed that it has no policies and procedures designed to address MEV. That sits alongside general protections, including permissioned participation, participant minimum-output settings, surveillance and the ability to revoke access for manipulative trading. OKXICE says those features mitigate, rather than eliminate, MEV risk.

Its planned pools use Uniswap v4 on XLayer, where the notice says the sequencer generally orders transactions according to offered network fees.

Perpetuals.com's notice proposes generally first-in, first-out sequencing, with priority for trading-halt instructions and signed arrival receipts intended to make ordering verifiable. Yet its affiliated sequencer would receive nonpublic transaction information.

A private pending-transaction route can reduce exposure to outsiders while leaving a concentrated information advantage to manage.

Its October notice describes safeguards that were not fully implemented and surveillance intended to precede trading. Formation's notice likewise describes planned safeguards for its Solana venue.

Preventing an abusive trade, detecting it afterward, and compensating its victim are different functions.

TSV says participants generally should not expect compensation for market or impermanent losses, compromised participant systems or losses caused by unaffiliated third parties, unless it expressly agrees otherwise or the law requires it.

Perpetuals.com reports no established compensation policy for the risks described in its notice. Formation specifically excludes compensation for lost or compromised private keys or wallets, which does not establish a general recovery guarantee for other losses.

Criminal proof, securities-venue duties and compensation terms address different parts of investor protection.

A disclosed safeguard is not a guarantee of prevention or repayment.

The acquittal concerns this prosecution. Venue notices include plans and operator statements, not independently tested controls. Compensation depends on the particular loss, applicable law and agreements; the graphic predicts no liability outcome.

Qualitative mapping of the court's material-misrepresentation finding, the SEC's MEV disclosure and preserved-law provisions, and disclosed venue compensation limits. Branches show distinct questions, not sequence or money flows.

The acquittal concerns this prosecution. Venue notices include plans and operator statements, not independently tested controls. Compensation depends on the particular loss, applicable law and agreements; the graphic predicts no liability outcome.

The SEC order recognizes that sequencing can support price-stabilizing activity as well as abuse. TSV says it does not intend to prevent lawful ordinary post-execution arbitrage or price alignment that avoids misuse of confidential information and other prohibited conduct.

The dividing line cannot simply be whether someone profits after another trade. Relevant distinctions include whether pending information was misused, how the venue controls ordering, what conduct it addresses, and which legal obligations apply.

The next disputed on-chain securities trade would raise questions about what representations were made, what rules applied, who controlled the information and ordering, and what recourse the investor had. Bringing stocks onto a blockchain makes those choices visible.

#5044. Hackers found a way to steal crypto without hacking wallets, and Ledger was caught in the attack

Malicious scripts found across 178 organizations could silently redirect cryptocurrency payments to attackers, exposing a threat that private-key security alone cannot prevent.

Ledger's website was among 178 organizations exposed to malicious scripts that could redirect cryptocurrency payments to hackers, researchers reported Monday.

Cybersecurity firm Report URI discovered the malware in 188 website scripts associated with major brands, including Ledger, BBC, CNN, Zoom, Salesforce and HSBC.

The firm examined 1,611 scripts delivered through Optimizely, a platform businesses use to test and personalize their websites. Attackers had inserted malicious instructions into customer-specific JavaScript, allowing infected code to reach visitors through a service those websites already trusted.

The attack exposed a vulnerability for cryptocurrency users: hackers could redirect funds without stealing private keys or compromising wallets.

Ledger's inclusion in the affected websites does not establish that its hardware wallets, firmware, or signing infrastructure were breached.

The malicious code was designed to identify crypto addresses displayed on websites and replace them with destinations controlled by attackers.

Researchers at Hudson Rock found that the malware could alter wallet addresses in visible text, input fields, links, and clipboard operations. It supported multiple blockchain networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, Tron, and Litecoin.

For example, a user copying a legitimate Bitcoin payment address from an infected page could unknowingly paste an attacker-controlled address into their wallet application.

If the user then approved the transaction without verifying the destination, the funds would go to the attacker.

The technique circumvents an important layer of cryptocurrency security because the wallet can function exactly as intended while authorizing a payment to the wrong recipient.

Hudson Rock found no evidence that the recovered malware directly extracted wallet seed phrases or initiated unauthorized blockchain transactions through wallet-signing functions.

Researchers described code that could alter a displayed or copied crypto address before a transfer, without needing the private key.

Illustrative flow for embedded-fallback variants: a host block may stop the fetch while local code remains available, subject to browser policy.

However, the attack appears to have generated financial proceeds.

Report URI identified approximately $7,040 received by attacker-linked crypto addresses as of 19:25 UTC on Nov. 16. Bitcoin accounted for roughly $5,380, while additional transactions involved Litecoin, Tron, Ethereum, and BNB Smart Chain.

Researchers also identified code associated with the Coruna and DarkSword iPhone exploit kits, potentially exposing visitors using vulnerable Apple devices to additional attacks.

These capabilities expanded the threat beyond crypto holders to customers interacting with infected websites.

Report URI said a large-scale cleanup reduced the number of infected scripts in its sample from 169 at 20:56 UTC to just one at 21:52 UTC on Nov. 16. Ledger's affected script was reportedly cleaned at approximately 20:41 UTC.

However, the researchers also discovered embedded copies of the malicious code, meaning blocking the attackers' remote servers alone might not eliminate every infection.

The central mystery is how the attackers got permission to modify scripts for so many unrelated organizations.

The researchers have not established whether the intrusion originated from compromised credentials, an Optimizely system, or another access route.

At publication, the investigation had not established the number of affected visitors or the full extent of financial losses, leaving the attackers' initial access method and the campaign's ultimate impact unresolved.

#5041. Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger has brought clear signing to Solana, allowing users of its hardware wallets to read exactly what they are approving before they sign a transaction.

"Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind," the company tweeted on Monday. Users can now verify a swap on decentralized exchange aggregator Jupiter "in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve," it said.

Would you approve a swap you can’t read?Clear Signing is finally live on Solana. Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind. Now you can verify a Jupiter swap, in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve.

Clear Signing now covers… pic.twitter.com/BuW3m7y5GG

Blind signing, in which users approve transactions displayed as unreadable data, has long been a weak point for hardware wallet users, since it leaves them unable to spot a malicious request. Ian Rogers, Ledger's chief human agency officer, likened it to "signing a blank check" in a presentation at Solana's Breakpoint conference.

A Jupiter swap that previously meant scrolling through 13 hard-to-read screens on a Ledger device now takes three, showing what the user is giving, what they're getting and who they're dealing with, Rogers said. "It's a huge step for every Solana user," he added.

Clear signing now covers nine Solana apps and 18 of the 22 most common actions users take on the network, according to Ledger. It was built with the Solana Foundation and Anza, the developer of Solana's Agave validator client, with Jupiter and lending protocol Kamino as the first supported apps.

Rogers also pitched the company's tools for AI agents. Ledger's approach is that an agent "never holds the keys; you do," he said, comparing it to a teenager who has to ask parents for the family car. The agent requests approval when it needs to move funds, and the user signs off on a Ledger device.

Because that means an agent has to stop and wait, Ledger last month launched Agent Intents, a single queue where approval requests collect so agents can keep working while users approve them when convenient. It works with Pay.sh, a service that lets AI agents pay for APIs on Solana, Rogers said.

He also showed off Ledger Atom, the touchscreen device based on Android's open-source code that Ledger announced last month. When it's time to sign, the device switches its screen to Ledger's secure chip, so secrets stay hidden from Android, Rogers said. Atom will be the first hardware beyond the Solana Seeker phone to carry the Seeker platform, bringing its more than 1,500 apps to the device, he added.

Ledger has previously teamed up with MoonPay on tools to let users control AI agents' crypto spending with its hardware wallets.

The launch comes weeks after Ledger investigated a suspected $87 million drain of user funds, which it later said was tied in all confirmed cases to a reseller, Cryptobilis.

#5038. Lighter’s exploit raises uncomfortable questions for Hyperliquid’s $5.7 billion market

The attack exposed how compromised trading keys can drain funds, raising concerns about what similar unauthorized activity could mean for larger exchanges.

Lighter's Nov. 16 security update described how exposed trading keys enabled an account drain through public-pool shares, putting a potential Hyperliquid exploit in perspective.

Lighter said that withdrawals had resumed after its safeguard allowed about 5 million USDC to be returned.

Hyperliquid has roughly $5.7 billion in open interest, representing about 55% of the open interest in the DefiLlama ranking as of Nov. 16.

Depending on the account permissions, a compromised agent could cause losses through unauthorized orders and liquidation in the accounts it can trade. Wider losses would depend on the affected positions, liquidity providers, and market depth.

Lighter said that affected accounts used unsafe signing code outside its official software kits. Reused cryptographic signing nonces allowed the attacker to reconstruct API private keys from public signatures, while the protocol, contracts, infrastructure, and official kits were unaffected.

The attacker created a public pool under the affected account, acquired shares through another account, then moved victim funds into the pool without creating corresponding shares. Redeeming the existing shares allowed funds to leave.

The account said wallet signatures were now required for public-pool transfers and that API signatures had been made non-public, with IP allowlists and configurable limits added as further protections.

Separately, Bitquery traced a 10,393,057 USDC withdrawal from Lighter's Ethereum contract on the same day. Its investigation could not inspect the internal rollup transactions or independently establish the reported second account loss.

Hyperliquid's API-wallet documentation says a master account can authorize agents to sign for itself or its subaccounts. Its official agent example describes trading authority without transfer or withdrawal permissions.

The exchange endpoint allows an agent-signed asset transfer when the destination matches the source address, an expressly permitted internal movement distinct from sending assets to another owner.

Hyperliquid also documents a vault-transfer function, and its official Python SDK uses a different signing path for it than for external USDC transfers and bridge withdrawals. The signing code alone does not establish which agent the exchange will authorize to request the exchange.

The derivatives venue's current overview describes customizable HyperEVM accounting and agent delegation, separately from legacy HyperCore vaults. Those distinctions prevent a blanket conclusion about every vault.

A comparable Lighter extraction would require both the relevant permissions and a matching share-accounting path. The documented internal transfers and vault functions leave no basis for a blanket assurance that every agent-authorized vault path is impossible.

A stolen agent with valid authority could open unwanted positions, change exposure, or cause adverse execution. The resulting losses could exhaust the affected account's collateral through trading and liquidation, without a withdrawal to the thief.

The Nov. 16 snapshot showed Hyperliquid open interest of $5.7 billion, against nearly $554 million for Lighter and a tracked total of roughly $10.5 billion. Hyperliquid was about 10.3 times Lighter's size on that measure and accounted for approximately 54.7% of the total.

DefiLlama's Hyperliquid and Lighter protocol pages reported the following activity and bridge snapshots on Nov. 16:

The first layer of a hypothetical agent compromise is the authorized account. The next layer appears if that account supplies substantial market liquidity: missing quotes or forced hedging could worsen execution for others. HLP losses would require relevant strategy or liquidation exposure.

Market depth also matters more than a venue-wide balance. DefiLlama's exchange snapshot showed about $1.4 billion of aggregate dollar-perpetual depth within 2%, with far less available in individual markets.

Cross-venue effects could develop through shared intermediaries, hedges, and collateral needs, but those connections would have to be established for the affected participants. Lighter's incident does not demonstrate that chain of events at Hyperliquid.

Hyperliquid's earlier episodes illustrate why a trader's loss, a vault drawdown and a platform interruption need separate treatment.

On March 12, 2025, approximately $4 million in HLP losses followed a large ETH liquidation, leading Hyperliquid to subsequently reduce maximum Bitcoin and Ethereum leverage to 40 times and 25 times.

The March 26 JELLY episode led to a reported loss of nearly $12 million and a later operator statement putting 24-hour HLP profit at roughly 700,000 USDC. As a response, validators delisted the market after suspicious activity.

There was also a November 2025 POPCAT incident that culminated in an estimated $4.9 million HLP loss and a temporary Arbitrum-bridge pause. The fourth episode happened on April 9, with an approximately $1.5 million daily HLP drawdown amid suspected FARTCOIN manipulation.

HLP's 

The clearest documented compensation process followed the July 29, 2025 API disruption. Hyperliquid's Aug. 4 announcement described 37 minutes of delayed orders and error responses even as transactions reached blocks, and its refund method covered specified fills and unusually high funding payments.

The process excluded the smallest calculated refunds and required business or identity verification for larger recipients to receive their full balance.

An Aug. 5 update said refunds had been distributed, describing a conditional response to one operational incident.

Lighter's token LIT weakened during the incident, dropping from $2.75 to $2.32 right after it, but recovering to $2.88 as of press time

A historical HYPE comparison from 2025 shows the token fell from $14.04 to $12.84 during the March 12 ETH-whale episode, before some recovery.

Relevant escalation signals for Hyperliquid would include an affected account's trading authority and positions, deterioration in the markets it supports, losses reaching HLP, or disruption to settlement and withdrawals.

Its $5.7 billion in open bets makes those distinctions consequential, but the dollar amount threatened by a particular compromise would still depend on the permissions, exposure, and liquidity involved.

#5034. How Bitcoin still competes with a 2.92% Treasury yield after inflation

The headline 10-year Treasury yield fell on Nov. 16, while its inflation-adjusted counterpart edged up and remained 29 basis points above its Nov. 6 level.

The 10-year Treasury yield after inflation rose to 2.92% on Monday, Nov. 16, up 29 basis points since Nov. 6. The move left Bitcoin competing with that yield even as the headline 10-year Treasury rate fell.

Treasury’s nominal yield data showed a decline to 5.12% from 5.15% on Friday, Nov. 13, a three-basis-point move. Its real-yield series moved the other way, edging up one basis point from 2.91%.

The real yield had stood at 2.63% on Nov. 6. The 29-basis-point rise therefore spans multiple sessions, with Monday adding only one basis point.

Bitcoin provides no contractual interest income. A higher yield on inflation-protected government debt can therefore raise the opportunity cost of holding it: investors have a stronger income alternative against which to weigh Bitcoin’s potential returns and volatility.

The 2.92% figure is also a long-term Treasury curve benchmark, not an overnight borrowing rate or a promised return for every investor. Treasury estimates its constant-maturity nominal and real par yields from indicative market quotations. An investor’s realized return on a particular bond can differ.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The November 16 bond rally lowered the nominal benchmark while the 10-year real yield reached 2.92%. Compare Monday’s small move with the rise since November 6.

Monday added one basis point to the real yield.

The real yield rose 29 basis points across multiple sessions.

The real-yield hurdle did not ease on Monday; its 29-basis-point rise spans November 6–16.

Curve estimates use indicative quotations. The calculated spread includes inflation risk and liquidity effects; it is not a pure forecast. The graphic establishes no Bitcoin price response. Comparisons are dated observations, not intraday moves.

Inputs are dated 10-year Treasury par yields. Spread = nominal minus real: 5.15−2.91=2.24 pp; 5.12−2.92=2.20 pp. Monday changes: real +1 bp, spread −4 bp, nominal −3 bp. Since Nov. 6: 2.92−2.63=0.29 pp=29 bp. Bars start at zero; both real-yield comparison views share a labelled 2.60%–3.00% scale.

Curve estimates use indicative quotations. The calculated spread includes inflation risk and liquidity effects; it is not a pure forecast. The graphic establishes no Bitcoin price response. Comparisons are dated observations, not intraday moves.

A simple subtraction of Treasury’s real par yield from its nominal par yield gives a spread of 2.20 percentage points on Monday, down from 2.24 on Friday. The four-basis-point narrowing explains how the nominal benchmark could fall while the real benchmark rose.

The spread is often described as inflation compensation. But the Federal Reserve’s explanation shows why it cannot be read as a pure inflation forecast: it also reflects compensation for inflation risk and differences in liquidity between nominal Treasuries and inflation-protected securities. The subtraction alone cannot show which component drove Monday’s change.

Historical research supports caution about the transmission to crypto. The authors of a 2023 IMF working paper found that U.S. monetary tightening reduced a broad crypto price factor through investor risk-taking. Their study does not estimate how Monday’s one-basis-point real-yield move affected Bitcoin.

Watch the inflation-adjusted Treasury yield for signs that competition from bonds is easing. The headline rate can fall while that yield stays elevated.

#5029. JPMorgan and DBS offer 24/7 dollar transfers… without stablecoins

Institutional clients can move dollars between the US and Singapore around the clock. One JPMorgan conversion step still pauses for three hours on Saturdays.

Singapore bank DBS and JPMorgan's blockchain business Kinexys have connected their blockchain-based dollar accounts in Singapore and the US, allowing institutional clients to transfer funds instantly around the clock as an alternative to stablecoins, according to DBS's Nov. 16 announcement.

The service gives corporate treasurers another way to move bank deposits beyond traditional banking hours.

Ant International is the first client of the commercially ready connection, which uses Partior to coordinate settlement between the banks' permissioned networks. The launch puts a practical service behind the interoperability framework the banks announced in November 2025.

The competitive opportunity for banks is to make deposits easier to move while preserving the customer relationship attached to them.

Stablecoins offer another way to transfer dollars digitally, but whether companies favor one route depends on where they can send the money, how they recover cash, and what the whole transaction costs.

A tokenized deposit represents a bank deposit, while USDC is issued by Circle affiliates and backed by cash and cash-equivalent reserves. Moving either on a blockchain does not make the customer's counterparty or redemption arrangements identical.

DBS says transfers within Kinexys's Blockchain Deposit Account network operate 24/7/365. However, moving money between those accounts and traditional demand-deposit accounts has a maintenance interruption every Saturday from 3 p.m. to 6 p.m. Eastern Time.

The banks did not disclose fees or how much cash Ant needs to keep on hand, so companies have no like-for-like cost comparison with stablecoin transfers.

Circle says standard banking-rail USDC redemptions can be free and typically settle in up to two business days, while qualifying Circle Platform and Circle Payments Network customers can obtain free near-real-time redemption.

Circle attributes fast redemption to cash pre-positioned at bank partners, which earns less than Treasuries, and its Mint product terms also make instant settlement conditional on participating banks offering it.

Direct Mint access is restricted to eligible institutions, while individuals obtain USDC through exchanges and wallets.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The DBS–Kinexys link connects bank accounts continuously. Access through traditional accounts and USDC redemption follows distinct rules.

Direct Circle Mint redemption is available to eligible institutions. Circle says free standard banking-rail redemptions typically settle in up to two business days.

Direct Circle Mint redemption is available to eligible institutions. Free near-real-time redemption requires qualifying Platform or CPN activity and participating-bank support.

Compare the full cash journey, including account access, redemption and eligibility.

DBS's Saturday pause applies to Kinexys entry/exit from traditional accounts, not the continuous cross-bank route. Direct Circle Mint redemption is for eligible institutions; near-real-time access has additional Platform/CPN and bank conditions. Neither diagram establishes prefunding savings or universal access.

Direct Circle Mint redemption is for eligible institutions; near-real-time access has additional Platform/CPN and bank conditions. Neither diagram establishes prefunding savings or universal access.

A Bank for International Settlements (BIS) working paper published Oct. 13 uses weekly US data from January 2019 through June 2025. Researchers Rashad Ahmed and Iñaki Aldasoro found that higher deposit rates were followed by slower stablecoin growth, and that the relationship was stronger for USDC than USDT.

The paper links a one-standard-deviation increase in deposit repricing to about 2.4 percentage points slower stablecoin growth over ten weeks. Stablecoin growth averaged 15% over a ten-week period in the sample.

The findings suggest that deposit pricing helps shape demand for stablecoins, but the authors say their analysis does not establish that stablecoin growth necessarily reduces total bank deposits or lending.

The DBS link is part of a wider effort to make bank money usable across more systems, though the products are distinct.

On Oct. 19, JPMorgan brought its JPM Coin deposit token to Ethereum, alongside Base, for institutional clients. That public-chain token is separate from the permissioned Blockchain Deposit Account network connected to DBS.

Using its internal data, JPMorgan reported more than $4 trillion in cumulative transactions and $7 billion in average daily volume across the broader Kinexys platform.

In September, DBS said it and Citi's New York office had completed a dollar payment via SWIFT Digital Ledger in minutes over a weekend.

Separately, The Clearing House selected Quant to support a tokenized-deposit network with connections to RTP and CHIPS. Participating institutions are expected to gain access in the first half of 2027.

A faster transfer inside one network only helps if the receiving bank can accept it.

The competition is consequently about more than whose blockchain runs all weekend. The decisive advantage for corporate cash would be money available where it is needed, with fewer costly balances waiting elsewhere.

#5028. Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

😱 Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

The Crypto Fear and Greed Index printed 10 on Sunday. A month ago it read 63, which is Greed. A week ago it was 28. It has been sliding every day since, and 10 is about as low as that gauge goes.

This weekend’s price action certainly was extremely fearful. Bitcoin touched roughly $62,000 before stabilizing in the mid-60s, down about a quarter from its early-October high and down something like 9% in three days. ETH fell under $1,850. Solana, XRP and dogecoin are all down more than 25% on the month. Bitcoin is now trading below its 200-week moving average, a level that has historically acted as the floor beneath every cycle, and it lost its 50-week average a week earlier.

It wasn’t just majors. Zcash fell to $800. Hype fell to $62. VVV went sub-$17. Nothing was spared.

The billion-dollar question of course—why is Bitcoin (and crypto) selling off so hard?

CZ addressed the question directly on Sunday, saying “No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation.” But he did give potential reasons.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

The reasons he listed: money rotating into the Anthropic IPO, ETF outflows, a run of security incidents including the Binance hack itself plus drained iPhone-linked wallets, a Chrome exploit and a Microsoft outage, hot US inflation raising the odds of another Fed hike and strengthening the dollar, and political uncertainty after midterm results that point toward stricter crypto policy.

Those are all pretty valid. Some of those overhangs remove soon (Anthropic IPO), some will fade over time (political uncertainty), but the cyber attack threat will remain until Bitcoin and broader crypto can present a real solution. And if we are faced with a hiking cycle in 2027 due to inflation, that will certainly be a continued headwind.

So where does this leave us? Nvidia reports Tuesday with an interim CEO while its former one openly campaigns to return. The Fed meets December 9 with inflation running hot enough to put another hike in play. And Bitcoin is sitting under a technical level that has held for three cycles.

Extreme fear has marked bottoms before. It has also marked the middle of declines. Now we wait for something other than sentiment to turn.

#5023. Hyperliquid’s $3.4 billion HYPE burn machine faces new test as USDC reserves shrink

Trading volume surged nearly 16%, but a 7.8% decline in USDC supply raises questions about the sustainability of a second source of HYPE buyback funding.

Hyperliquid's perpetual futures trading volume surged nearly 16% over the past week, even as the network's USDC holdings declined, exposing competing trends in the revenue streams supporting HYPE token buybacks.

Data from DeFiLlama showed that the decentralized exchange processed $75.72 billion in perpetual futures transactions over the seven days ending Nov. 16, an increase of 15.87%.

However, USDC capitalization on the network fell 7.76% to $6.02 billion during the same period. The stablecoin accounted for approximately 98.85% of Hyperliquid's total stablecoin supply.

The divergence puts Hyperliquid's token-buyback model in focus, particularly as the protocol increasingly relies on both trading fees and income generated from stablecoin reserves to support HYPE purchases.

Hyperliquid has already accumulated about 49.03 million HYPE tokens through its Assistance Fund, representing 4.9% of the cryptocurrency's initial one-billion-token supply.

Under the protocol's rules, the holdings were treated as burned and were valued at about $3.37 billion as of Nov. 17. Their estimated acquisition cost was $1.45 billion, with an average purchase price near $29.50 per token.

Hyperliquid's fee structure directs trading revenue toward liquidity providers, market deployers, and the Assistance Fund, which automatically converts its allocated fees into HYPE.

Weekly turnover rose while chain-wide USDC fell. The routes to HYPE purchases depend on different balances, rates and timing.

Actual fee receipts and reserve payments are the comparable inputs for assessing HYPE funding.

Chain-wide USDC is not the qualifying treasury balance. The retained sources do not reconcile current eligible daily balances, effective AQA rates or November payments. The timeline shows the recurring rule, not the implementation grace period or a specific payment.

Uses the November 16 reported seven-day dashboard figures and the documented fee and AQAv2 mechanisms. Backing split drawn 9:1; schedule lengths drawn 30:8. Route widths do not encode dollars; no income estimate or current rate is calculated.

Chain-wide USDC is not the qualifying treasury balance. The retained sources do not reconcile current eligible daily balances, effective AQA rates or November payments. The timeline shows the recurring rule, not the implementation grace period or a specific payment.

The accumulated tokens are permanently removed from the circulating supply, linking exchange activity directly to demand for its native cryptocurrency.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

This mechanism has become an important component of HYPE's token economics, with the protocol using revenue from its trading business to steadily accumulate tokens.

The latest increase in perpetuals volume strengthens one side of that arrangement by expanding the trading activity from which fees are collected.

However, the amount reaching the Assistance Fund also depends on transaction fee tiers, staking discounts, maker rebates and revenue-sharing arrangements with market deployers.

Hyperliquid has also expanded its funding model beyond trading fees through AQAv2, an arrangement that redirects income earned on eligible USDC reserves toward the protocol.

Under the framework, Hyperliquid receives approximately 90% of cost-adjusted reserve yield, providing another source of funds for HYPE purchases.

Coinbase serves as the treasury operator, while Circle provides technical support for the stablecoin infrastructure.

Unlike trading fees, which depend on transaction activity, this income is tied to qualifying USDC balances and prevailing interest rates.

That creates a potential vulnerability as stablecoin capitalization declines, even while activity on the exchange strengthens.

The importance of Hyperliquid's stablecoin reserves became more apparent in the third quarter, when the network began generating substantial income through the AQAv2 arrangement.

According to Bitwise and Allium, the protocol accrued approximately $17.26 million in USDC reserve income during the quarter, including $14.86 million in September.

At the end of September, Hyperliquid held approximately $7.35 billion in total USDC, with $6.77 billion backing HyperCore, its trading infrastructure. Of that amount, the treasury held approximately $6.09 billion.

Using those balances and a 3.22% annual reference rate, Bitwise estimated the arrangement could generate about $16.09 million over 30 days, or nearly $196 million annually.

That potential income adds another source of demand for HYPE, reinforcing the buybacks already funded by trading fees.

The latest contraction in USDC capitalization threatens to weaken that contribution if the decline extends to the qualifying reserves on which the income is earned.

The reserve arrangement calculates revenue from daily balances and interest rates over 30-day periods, with payments reaching the Assistance Fund eight days after each period ends.

Consequently, shrinking eligible reserves would gradually reduce the income available for HYPE purchases, while stronger trading activity could help compensate through higher fee collections.

For Hyperliquid, the emerging tension is between an increasingly active derivatives business and a contracting stablecoin base that supports a separate source of buyback funding.

The next reserve-income distribution will provide a clearer picture of whether Hyperliquid can sustain this additional source of HYPE demand as USDC balances fall.

#5018. 'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

Former UK Prime Minister Boris Johnson is sticking to his doubts about Bitcoin, telling a room full of crypto developers that he still can't see who is in charge of the world's largest cryptocurrency.

"As far as I can see, nobody knows who's in charge, apart from Yakimoto Nakasori, and I don't know where to find him," Johnson said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London, deliberately mangling the name of Bitcoin's pseudonymous creator, Satoshi Nakamoto. "If six billion got stolen from some cryptocurrency, I wouldn't know who to apply to, or how to get the money back. That's my problem."

Johnson was asked about a column he wrote in March for the Daily Mail, in which he called Bitcoin a "giant Ponzi scheme." The column drew pushback from the crypto industry, including Strategy executive chairman Michael Saylor, who tweeted that Bitcoin has no issuer, promoter or guaranteed return.

Bitcoin is not a Ponzi scheme. A Ponzi requires a central operator promising returns and paying early investors with funds from later ones. Bitcoin has no issuer, no promoter, and no guaranteed return—just an open, decentralized monetary network driven by code and market demand.

— Michael Saylor (@saylor) March 13, 2026

On stage, Johnson admitted he is "not a great crypto buff" and holds little, if any, crypto himself. His view was shaped by a man he met in his Oxfordshire village who had lost thousands and kept paying more in the hope of recovering his losses, he said. "If the search for the greater fool had reached the wilds of South Oxfordshire, then probably there was something not quite right about this thing," Johnson said.

He also questioned the name itself, noting that "crypto" comes from the Greek for "hidden." "I don't think it's a good sell to tell people that this thing is concealed," he said.

Johnson drew a sharp line between Bitcoin and the technology behind it. "Please don't think that my Luddite anxiety about crypto in any way means that I wish to slow down the development of blockchain, or the lead that London should rightly have in that sort of technology," he said. When it comes to blockchain and tokenizing assets, the UK has "got to be at the forefront," he added.

Moderator Gareth Jenkinson, head of multimedia at The Block, pushed back when Johnson suggested Bitcoin was valuable to drug dealers, arguing that blockchains are far more traceable than cash. Asked by Johnson who guarantees Bitcoin, Jenkinson replied: "No one has to guarantee it. That's the beauty of the protocol."

Lord Ross Kempsell, a Conservative member of the House of Lords who previously worked for Johnson, said more than 8% of UK adults now hold some form of crypto asset, and that institutional adoption of blockchains is growing in the City of London. That ownership figure has fallen, however: the Financial Conduct Authority's latest consumer research, conducted in August and September 2025, found 8% of UK adults own crypto, down from 12% in 2024, though still double the level in 2021.

The UK is "on the precipice of creating a lot of regulation" for the sector, Kempsell said, pointing to the FCA's work on its regulatory perimeter and the Bank of England's coming stablecoin framework. "I share Boris's anxieties about the risks," he said, but within that perimeter, "there's a lot of scope here for a high growth industry."

In June, the FCA finalized its rulebook for crypto firms ahead of a mandatory regime due to take effect in late 2027, days after the Bank of England eased its stablecoin rules, scrapping planned individual holding caps in favor of a £40 billion issuance limit per coin. The Bank has since been handed a legal duty to foster stablecoin innovation, and it has set out a shared vision for tokenization with the FCA.

Kempsell also argued that being outside the EU's Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulation allows the UK to set its own rules for crypto. The EU itself is set to revise MiCA in 2027 to cover foreign stablecoin issuers.

#5014. Standard Chartered says Japan’s bond tokenization plans could help drive RWA market to $2 trillion

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The bank said tokenizing Japanese government bonds could bring more long-term debt onchain and encourage other countries to follow.

Japan's plans to tokenize government bonds (JGBs) could help the tokenized real-world asset market reach Standard Chartered Bank's existing forecast of $2 trillion by the end of 2028.

The market currently stands at around $45 billion, Geoffrey Kendrick, the bank's global head of digital assets research, wrote in a Monday report. Its $2 trillion forecast has mainly relied on tokenized money market funds and equities, each growing to $750 billion. Longer-dated government bonds would provide another source of growth toward that target, Kendrick said.

"The current crypto sell-off will pass, and when it does, it will be the bringing of real-world assets on-chain that matters most," Kendrick said in an email shared with The Block.

"The more important implication of tokenizing longer-dated debt in one of the world's largest government bond markets is that others may follow suit," Kendrick wrote in the report.

Most government debt brought onchain so far has been short-term Treasury bills, with almost no longer-dated debt tokenized, according to the report. Kendrick put tokenized U.S. Treasury bills at around $13 billion, compared with just under $1 billion in tokenized government debt outside the U.S.

Japan has the world's third-largest government bond market, and BlackRock recently signed a tokenization MOU with SBI and Startale there. Kendrick said other governments may follow if Japan's program becomes large enough to demonstrate cost savings and easier movement of bonds as collateral.

Japan's Ministry of Finance held its first study group meeting on bringing government bonds onchain on Oct. 8. Kendrick noted that there are three options under consideration. Japan could tokenize existing government bonds, issue new bonds on a distributed ledger, or do both.

The first option would be simpler because the legal structure and cash flows would remain the same, Kendrick said. Issuing bonds directly on a ledger could save more work and costs. Japan is still gathering industry feedback.

"While the plan still lacks key details, such as the choice of blockchain and timelines, we think the proposal is a significant step forward for one of the world's largest government bond markets," Kendrick wrote.

Several pilot programmes to tokenize government debt are underway around the world, most notably the UK's Digital Gilt Instrument pilot, expected to go live in early 2027, Kendrick noted. He said a large Japanese program could encourage the U.S. and other major government bond markets to pursue tokenization too, following Wall Street's emerging tokenization model, adding: "This would help to drive overall tokenised RWAs towards our end-2028 market cap forecast of USD 2tn."
