#4920. Binance hack may push institutions to outside custody, FalconX exec says

FalconX’s Joshua Lim says the Binance hack could push institutions to keep crypto off exchanges and change how large investors trade in 2027.

A major shift in institutional crypto trading could be on the horizon following the recent Binance hack, as security concerns may lead to more large investors keeping their assets outside exchanges, according to a FalconX executive.

In an exclusive interview with Cointelegraph at the Digital Asset Forum in New York City on Nov. 13, Joshua Lim, global head of markets at FalconX, a digital asset brokerage for institutional investors, said the Oct. 29 hack could make institutions more cautious about trading crypto and change how they store their assets.

“It’s actually quite bearish for more institutional participation in crypto,” Lim told Cointelegraph. He said traders initially played down the hack but later began to recognize its potential long-term impact.

As a result, Lim expects more institutions to turn to outside custody providers and trading firms that allow them to trade without keeping their crypto on exchanges, a shift he believes could become one of the biggest trends in 2027.

North Korean-linked hackers stole about $6.4 billion in cryptocurrency from Binance, making it the largest crypto theft on record, according to blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis.

The shift toward keeping crypto outside exchanges already appeared to be underway even before the hack. In an October proposal, the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said industry practices were developing that reduced the need to move investment advisers’ client assets and regulated funds’ crypto holdings out of permitted custody for trading.

Lim pointed to Copper, a crypto custody provider, as one company that could benefit. Its ClearLoop service lets investors trade on supported exchanges while keeping their crypto with a separate company.

Copper said collateral protected by its ClearLoop service supported more than $240 billion in perpetual futures trading volume during the first quarter of 2026, more than double the amount a year earlier.

Lim explained that investors can keep their assets outside an exchange while receiving credited balances that allow them to trade on it. Under the brokerage model Lim described, a hedge fund can place trades through FalconX, which executes them using its own exchange accounts and relationships. Still, investors face risks tied to the firms holding their assets and settling their trades.

“For many players, facing a regulated entity like ours is more palatable,” Lim added.

Related: Crypto hack losses equal 57 cents per $1 earned onchain: Ark’s Valente

Binance recorded large Bitcoin outflows in mid-November, although it is not clear whether they were linked to the hack.

According to CryptoQuant data, Binance recorded net withdrawals of about 2,771 Bitcoin (BTC) on Friday, followed by 2,708 BTC on Saturday and 5,636 BTC on Sunday — totaling about 11,115 BTC leaving the exchange than entered over those three days.

The withdrawals came as the broader crypto market was falling, with Bitcoin trading around $65,000 at 9:41 pm UTC Monday, down about 20% over the past month, according to CoinGecko.

However, the data does not show who moved the Bitcoin or why. Investors could have transferred their funds to personal wallets, companies that store crypto on their behalf, or to other exchanges.

Beyond concerns about exchange security, Lim expects other changes in how large investors trade in 2027. He said institutions want more ways to trade assets such as oil, gold and stock market indexes through crypto platforms, including outside regular market hours.

“You can respond quickly to news events when they happen,” Lim said, citing one of the benefits of 24/7 trading.

That type of trading is already taking place on Hyperliquid, a decentralized trading platform that offers contracts tied to traditional assets. As of Monday, Hyperliquid’s markets for traditional assets and other contracts had about $4.3 billion in open interest and recorded $3.9 billion in trading volume over 24 hours, according to data from Perpys.

But while crypto platforms are giving investors more ways to trade, Lim said the financial services needed to support those trades have not kept up, particularly when it comes to borrowing money and managing risk.

For example, investors can now trade oil through traditional futures markets, investment funds and crypto platforms. But lenders may treat trades on each platform separately, even when those trades balance each other out.

“There’s not enough players that can give you leverage or margin financing against all of these things collectively,” Lim said. “The risk netting is not quite there yet.”

#4919. Can AI Companions Really Solve Loneliness?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Ekua Hagan

Engagement with artificial intelligence seems to be everywhere today, from education to medicine to business to making day-to-day life easier. Undoubtedly, some uses will prove beneficial, and others harmful. Evaluating the effect of different forms of AI engagement on human flourishing will thus be critical, and we believe our flourishing measure provides a helpful framework by which to do so. The effects of AI on flourishing may also differ across domains of flourishing.

There may be incredible potential for AI to advance human happiness, health, and financial security. It is not guaranteed to do so, and indeed, as per Pope Leo XIV’s recent encyclical Magnifica Humanitas (Magnificent Humanity), much of what AI does or does not achieve will depend upon whether the human person and human dignity are made central in the use and diffusion of AI. However, if employed well and equitably, the potential for AI to advance these aspects of human well-being may be substantial.

The effects, however, of AI technologies on more humanistic aspects of well-being, such as meaning, character, or relationships, seem more ambiguous. Rich, developed countries already seem to be struggling with these more humanistic aspects of well-being, possibly in part due to technology. Will AI help or hinder our sense of meaning, our character, our relationships? As always, so much depends on how we use it.

AI technologies with various relational elements have effectively been used for purposes ranging from helping autistic children develop social skills to building the capacity of university students for civil discourse. While such uses arguably always ought to point back to real relationships, these systems may indeed have the potential to truly enhance human relationships.

However, there is another class of chatbots that aims not at enhancing human relationships, but at replacing them. These are chatbots whose principal aim and purpose is friendship and romance. In a recent article, we have argued that such friendship and romance chatbots have no place in a flourishing life. This may seem like an extreme position. After all, what harm is there in having loneliness somewhat relieved if one’s friends or family are proving disappointing?

The problem is that the temporary alleviation of loneliness is not a solution to loneliness. Loneliness, as painful as it can be, is experienced precisely to try to direct our attention, and our longings, towards deeper human connection. That can take a lot of work and time, and may not be fully within our grasp. But that thirst for human connection has a fundamental role in our life, in guiding us towards others.

When AI chatbots have, as their goal, not helping us with human relationships, but replacing them, they will do damage—damage to ourselves, damage to those we might have interacted with, and damage to the social fabric of society. If they satisfy our loneliness, they do so without another person. A chatbot that always seems to understand, that never has needs of its own, that treats neglect with a delight to see us, ultimately makes us weaker in our capacity for real human relationships. We come to have unrealistic expectations of what to hope for in real human relationships. We lose the capacity to grow through the very difficulties and frictions that real human relationships entail. We come to focus on ourselves rather than on providing for the needs of others. Indeed, with AI friendship and romance, there is no one on the other side to whose life we have contributed.

While AI may have some capacity to enhance our relationships (pointing us towards helpful books or resources for particular problems we are wrestling with) and while certain AI chatbot applications may benefit from relational elements (like the training programs above), this is very different from AI chatbots aimed at replacing real relationships. The effects of these are universally and comprehensively detrimental. They harm us, and they harm those around us. Even if we do not feel the negative effects now, or if they seem small, the increasing incapacity for social relationships will over time gradually erode human connection, human community, and the fabric of society. Human relationships are critical for our health and well-being.

In a remarkable turn of events, China, which has been very pro-AI, in July put into place comprehensive legislation restricting the development and marketing of AI tools “that provide emotional care, companionship, support, and other emotional services,” and including a strict ban on tools “excessively pandering to users, inducing emotional reliance or addiction, [or] harming users' relationships with real people.” What does China know that we don’t? They seem to have almost presciently anticipated the likely effects of AI romance and relationships on human society, human relationships, human reproduction, and human community.

There have also recently been some helpful legislative efforts here in the United States, especially for minors. However, we are naïve to think children are the only ones at risk.

While we do think a ban on AI “relationship chatbots” for friendship and romance may be reasonable here (after all, we ban heroin in the United States), some might view such measures as too extreme. Even if so, we nevertheless believe every effort should be made in practice to purge AI friendship and romance from our lives as individuals and as communities. A technology designed to replace human connection is not a neutral technology.

Love—including love of neighbor and even love of enemy—is crucial for the flourishing of our society. In the words of Pope Leo (and others before him), if we are to seek a flourishing society (and to do so in the age of AI), we need to build “a civilization of love.” We need to love and care for one another. We need to be with one another. We need to have the capacity to work through the difficulties and frictions that real relationships entail, and to grow through those struggles. Therein lies our flourishing, for ourselves, for others, and for society. Real human love requires real human people.

Teubner, J.D. and VanderWeele, T.J. (2026). "Time to Ban AI Companions." First Things, 368:9-10. Retrieved from: https://firstthings.com/time-to-ban-ai-companions/

#4918. Judge Acquits MIT Brothers in $25M Ethereum MEV Case, Ending Prosecution

Judge Acquits MIT Brothers in $25M Ethereum MEV Case, Ending Prosecution

A federal judge in Manhattan has acquitted Anton and James Peraire-Bueno, the MIT-educated brothers accused of exploiting Ethereum's transaction ordering system to take $25 million from automated trading bots.

U.S. District Judge Jessica Clarke granted the brothers' motion for a judgment of acquittal on Monday, clearing them of wire fraud, conspiracy to commit wire fraud and conspiracy to commit money laundering, according to her ruling. The decision closes the criminal case without a second trial.

"The question before the Court is not whether the Peraire-Buenos' conduct was right or moral, or even whether it violated uncharged crimes," Clarke wrote. To convict on wire fraud, prosecutors had to show the brothers made a material misrepresentation, and none of the government's theories did so, she found. Ethereum, she noted, is a "trustless, highly adversarial environment with no terms of use."

While the brothers' scheme "may have frustrated the spirit" of MEV-Boost, software that lets Ethereum validators outsource the ordering of transactions to specialized block builders, that was not enough to make out fraud, Clarke said. In a footnote, she added that the prosecution "raises serious due process concerns," though she declined to rule on that question.

Because the money laundering charge relied on the proceeds of the alleged wire fraud, it fell with the fraud counts.

The case stemmed from an April 2023 exploit targeting so-called sandwich bots, automated traders that profit from maximal extractable value (MEV) by placing their own trades on either side of other users' transactions.

According to the ruling, the brothers placed bait transactions designed to lure specific bots, then exploited a bug in a relay's code to see the bots' bundled trades before committing to a block. They then swapped the bait transactions for trades that sold the tokens instead, leaving the bots with about $25 million in losses in roughly 12 seconds. Authorities arrested the pair in May 2024, saying they spent months planning the attack and took steps to hide the proceeds.

At trial in Manhattan last year, an insider testified that the brothers had allegedly planned the attack, and the defense had earlier sought to block evidence of their Google searches. Clarke declared a mistrial in November 2025, after the jury deadlocked.

The case was closely watched in the crypto industry. Clarke's ruling described it as a "first of its kind" prosecution, a label the government itself used in its indictment.

#4915. German lawmakers back crypto freezes outside criminal cases

Customs authorities could hold “suspicious” assets for 30 days under the bill, with court oversight for extensions and disputed confiscation.

Germany’s lower house of parliament has passed a bill that would let customs authorities temporarily freeze cryptocurrency and other high-value assets suspected of having unlawful origins without first opening a criminal investigation.

The Bundestag passed the customs reform bill on Friday as part of a wider effort to combat money laundering and organized crime. The proposed procedure would target assets worth more than 100,000 euros and explicitly mentions cryptocurrencies.

Under the bill’s text approved by lawmakers, authorities would be able to hold assets for up to 30 days where there are factual indications of unlawful origins, according to a translated version. However, filing an application for confiscation could suspend those deadlines pending a court decision, and in contested cases, a court would have to be satisfied that the assets were of unlawful origin before ordering confiscation, effectively putting the burden of proof on the crypto holder.

German Social Democratic Party lawmaker Ingo Vogel said that the powers would allow authorities to pursue suspicious assets even when there are not yet grounds to open a criminal case, according to the Bundestag’s summary of the debate on the bill.

The bill still requires consent from the Bundesrat, Germany’s upper house of parliament, and is intended to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

Germany’s Federal Bar Association, known as BRAK, previously opposed the government’s draft in an Oct. 28 statement, warning that the meaning of unlawful origin is too vague and risks treating missing records as evidence of illicit wealth.

The association pointed to assets inherited through generations as one example where owners may lack a paper trail. It also questioned what the administrative process would add to Germany’s existing criminal confiscation rules.

German blockchain industry association Bundesblock also raised concerns about the treatment of digital assets in a March 31 submission on an earlier draft. It warned at the time that blockchain analysis should not automatically be treated as proof of ownership or control, and that incorrect wallet attribution could even infringe fundamental rights.

Related: Crypto adoption blooming in Germany, while UK is falling ‘behind,’ says CoinShares researcher

Germany has already carried out large crypto seizures under existing law. As Cointelegraph reported, Saxony authorities sold 49,858 Bitcoin (BTC) linked to the Movie2k piracy investigation in 2024, raising about 2.6 billion euros, or $2.8 billion. At the time, prosecutors said the proceeds remained secured pending a court ruling on confiscation.

In a recent development, local outlet MDR reported on Friday that a Leipzig judge proposed ending proceedings against the main defendant in exchange for 40,000 BTC and a payment of 38 million euros for the potential victims. The proposal remains under consideration.

#4914. Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing back Jensen Huang as CEO, despite a growing campaign for his return led by some of the biggest names in tech and backed by President Donald Trump.

The board is focused on its investigation into Huang and is not in discussions to reinstate him, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC on Monday. The independent investigation, led by law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, is examining Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.

Huang resigned as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, after 33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded. His departure followed his admission of an affair and reports that he had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at an Nvidia customer whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, as well as with Nvidia employees. The week of revelations saw hundreds of billions of dollars wiped from Nvidia's market value.

The report cuts against a weekend of growing pressure for Huang's return. The Financial Times reported that Huang had discussed a comeback with allies, though Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner, one of the investors urging the board to keep "all options" open, told CNBC that Huang had not discussed returning with him. A representative for Huang declined to comment on the speculation.

An online petition calling for Huang's reinstatement had drawn 191,996 signatures by Monday. Its backers include Elon Musk, who said on Friday that he was signing it, as well as Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney. The site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. Nvidia employees have also pushed internally for his return.

Trump said on Monday that he would welcome Huang back. "I would certainly not mind seeing that," he told reporters, adding that national security had not been put at risk, as Decrypt reported. That puts him at odds with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO.

Others in tech have stopped short of backing a return. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, who was briefly ousted from his own company in 2023, said at a Semafor conference on Monday that Huang had "long been a hero of mine," but that he did not know enough to say whether Huang should come back.

Nvidia shares closed down 4.1% at $156.14 on Monday, and were down a further 0.5% at $155.67 in premarket trading early on Tuesday, according to Yahoo! Finance data.

Nvidia's board said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the CEO role. Chief financial officer Colette Kress, the interim CEO, will lead the company's quarterly earnings call on Tuesday.

#4912. What Is the Strongest Predictor of Divorce?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

How do you avoid getting divorced? By definition, all married couples seek to avoid their marriage falling apart. In wedding ceremonial vows, couples make commitments to each other, which typically include standing by one another’s side in sickness and health, and in times of prosperity and poverty. Marriage is meant to last forever, but it doesn’t always work out as intended. In the United States, 43-46 percent of marriages end in divorce, and that number rises for second or third marriages.1

Divorce is painful and costly. Getting divorced increases one’s risk of experiencing financial strain, drug and alcohol abuse, suicide, poorer health, and interrupted parenting if the couple has children. Despite the high prevalence of divorce and the substantial consequences associated with it, research on why marriages end has largely developed in separate camps: one focused on how couples communicate, another on the personal vulnerabilities each spouse brings into the marriage, and a third on couples’ financial and socioeconomic circumstances. These perspectives had never been directly compared to see which best predicts divorce. Recently, a team of social scientists published an article digging deep into this question.2

The researchers began by recruiting 368 heterosexual newlywed couples from the Los Angeles Project on Newlywed Development who had gotten married within the past 6 months. The study began in 2009-10 and concluded in 2021-22. Over these 12 years, the researchers tracked a variety of metrics from the couples and, of primary interest, which couples got divorced (or were separated). Six key predictor variables were collected, which include:

The vulnerability variables included a score composed of adverse childhood experiences, history of physical/sexual abuse, problematic substance use (past 9 months), negative affect (past 30 days), and parents’ marital status. Communication style was assessed by video recording couples’ discussion of a topic or issue in their relationship, which was carefully coded by trained research assistants. Socioeconomic status included typical metrics of education, income, work stability, financial strain, and neighborhood median income. Worth noting is that the sample itself was not necessarily representative of the general U.S. population (lower income than average, largely Hispanic).

By the end of the study, 39 percent of couples were divorced. When all six of the key predictor variables listed above were used together to estimate who got divorced, the evidence pointed to the wife’s vulnerabilities as the only statistically significant predictor of divorce. The wife’s vulnerabilities also predicted a secondary variable measuring how quickly couples ended their marriage. In other words, the more of these vulnerabilities a wife reported (adverse childhood experiences, physical/sexual abuse, problematic substance use, frequent negative emotions in the top 25 percent of the sample, or not being raised by married parents), the more likely the couple was to divorce, even after accounting for the other five predictors.

Although speculative, the authors propose a few reasons why this finding occurred. Women tend to handle the lion’s share of domestic and emotional labor in marriages. Women also tend to be more responsive to partners’ needs, have better memory and awareness of relationship events, and notice problems more often. In sum, women with more vulnerabilities may be more likely to reach the decision to exit more often and more quickly.

But are vulnerabilities deterministic of divorce? Not quite. The same study found that communication mattered in an important way. Among couples who communicated especially well, including more warmth, humor, and effective problem solving, and less hostility, the added divorce risk tied to wives’ vulnerabilities largely disappeared. The baggage women carry into a marriage appears real, but strong interpersonal communication seems to buffer against it. The findings point to two places worth investing in: reducing childhood adversity in the first place and offering couples support that works with those personal histories. Knowing where the risk lies doesn’t seal a couple’s fate. It tells them where to put in the work.

1. Amato, P. R. (2010). Research on divorce: Continuing trends and new developments. Journal of Marriage and Family, 72(3), 650–666.

2. Smith, M. G., Block, J. M., Bradbury, T. N., & Karney, B. R. (2026). Individual, interpersonal, and socioeconomic predictors of divorce: A 12-year prospective study. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology. Advance online publication. https://dx.doi.org/10.1037/pspi0000537

#4909. Custodia Bank loses Supreme Court bid for direct Fed payment access

The Supreme Court refused to hear Custodia Bank’s appeal, meaning a lower court ruling allowing the Fed to reject account applications will stand.

The United States Supreme Court on Monday declined to hear an appeal from crypto-focused bank Custodia, effectively ending the bank’s latest legal effort to gain direct access to the Federal Reserve’s payment system.

Custodia is a Wyoming-chartered bank founded by Caitlin Long. The bank has been trying for a Fed master account since 2020, which would allow it to process payments directly without relying on third-party financial institutions.

Now, the Supreme Court’s Monday decision means an earlier ruling allowing the Fed to reject applications from eligible banks will remain in effect.

The case highlights some of the challenges crypto-facing banks face in gaining direct access to the US payment system. Still, even if state-chartered banks qualify for master accounts, the Fed does not have to approve every application.

In a dissent issued in March, US Circuit Judge Timothy Tymkovich criticized the appeals court’s decision to give Federal Reserve banks discretion over master accounts.

“Without a master account, a bank cannot operate in the modern banking system,” Tymkovich wrote, arguing that the ruling effectively gives the Fed the power to override state banking decisions.

Custodia first sued the Federal Reserve in 2022 after waiting around two years for a decision on its master account application. The Federal Reserve Bank of Kansas City ended up rejecting the request in January 2023, citing concerns about risks linked to the bank’s crypto-focused business model.

In October 2025, a federal appeals court ruled against Custodia, finding that Federal Reserve banks did not have to grant master accounts to every eligible institution.

Related: Crypto among sectors ‘debanked’ by 9 major banks: US regulator

Cointelegraph previously reported that the court declined to reconsider its decision in March. This led Custodia to take its case to the Supreme Court.

The bank’s appeal also received support from the Blockchain Association, which argued that the Fed’s ability to reject applications could weaken the authority of state banking regulators.

Senator Cynthia Lummis and former Senator Patrick Toomey also filed a brief with the Supreme Court in August, supporting Custodia’s appeal.

Today’s decision comes as other crypto-focused banks seek access to the Fed’s payment system. In March, Kraken Financial received approval for a restricted account from the Federal Reserve Bank of Kansas City — the same regional bank that rejected Custodia’s application.

Notably, Kraken’s account was approved for an initial one-year period and allows it to send and receive payments through the Fed’s Fedwire system. However, the account has restrictions and does not offer all the services of a full master account.

Looking ahead, Custodia may still be able to apply for another master account or seek limited access to the Fed’s payment system under a separate proposal.

#4908. Optimizely Hack Hijacked Ledger, BBC and 176 Other Sites to Swap Crypto Wallet Addresses

Optimizely Hack Hijacked Ledger, BBC and 176 Other Sites to Swap Crypto Wallet Addresses

An attacker hijacked Optimizely, a platform widely used by companies to run experiments on their websites, to serve malware that swapped visitors' crypto wallet addresses for their own, on sites including hardware wallet maker Ledger.

Optimizely said in a status update on Monday evening that an unauthorized party had exploited a vulnerability in its Experimentation product to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects. The company said it had fixed the flaw, removed the injected code, secured affected user accounts and added protections to stop unauthorized code being delivered.

Earlier today, Optimizely identified and contained a security incident affecting some Optimizely Web Experimentation customers. The vulnerable path is blocked, the injected code was removed, and customers have been contacted directly.

— Optimizely (@Optimizely) November 17, 2026

Researchers at security firm Report URI, which sells tools to detect malicious website scripts, found the malicious code in 188 of the 1,611 Optimizely scripts they examined, belonging to 178 organizations, according to a blog post by founder Scott Helme. The affected sites included Zoom, the BBC, CNN, Salesforce, GitLab, IKEA and HSBC, as well as crypto and trading firms such as Ledger, IG and OANDA.

Because the code was served from Optimizely's own trusted servers, affected websites loaded it without their own systems being breached.

The malware's main purpose was to steal crypto. The script scanned web pages for valid wallet addresses on networks including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Tron, Solana and TON, and replaced them with addresses controlled by the attacker, according to Report URI. It also swapped addresses copied to a user's clipboard, meaning anyone who copied an address while an affected page was open could have pasted the attacker's instead.

The attacker's wallets received about $7,150 in crypto, mostly in Bitcoin, Litecoin and USDT, according to Report URI's update on Tuesday. The attacker emptied the wallets early on Tuesday. One Tron address used to cash out had also been used in earlier operations in March and on November 3, allowing Report URI to link the attacker to other attacks, the firm said.

The script also skimmed card details typed into some sites' own payment forms, though not those entered into hosted payment fields such as Stripe's, and on iPhones and iPads it attempted to load exploit kits known as Coruna and DarkSword, which target older versions of iOS. Report URI said it could not see how many card numbers were stolen or how many devices were compromised.

The injected code first appeared early on November 16 UTC, and had been removed from all of the scripts Report URI could find by late that evening.

Optimizely spokesperson Victoria Johnson told Gizmodo that an unauthorized party had "exploited a vulnerability in the Experimentation account signup flow," and that the company had blocked access to the vulnerable path, deployed a fix and notified all affected customers. The company has not said how the attacker gained access to other customers' projects.

Helme said visitors to affected sites on November 16 should check any crypto transfers they made, update their iPhones and iPads, and watch their card statements. Website operators should review custom code in their Optimizely projects and rotate credentials, he added.

#4906. The Spice Rack Brain Hack

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Ekua Hagan

“He who controls the spice controls the universe.”—Frank Herbert

Spice adds variety to your life. Especially in your microbiome. Studies are increasingly finding that spices can boost the variety of good microbes in your gut. That, in turn, can improve your mood and cognition via the gut-brain axis.

A study by Regina Lamendella and colleagues, published in The Journal of Nutrition, found that blends of herbs and spices—including cinnamon, ginger, cumin, turmeric, rosemary, oregano, basil, and thyme—increased diversity of the microbiome, a measure of health. The best results were with the participants who got the largest dose of spices—about 1 ½ teaspoons a day.

What is so great about spices and herbs? Their main attraction is their high levels of polyphenols—plant chemicals that provide flavor and color. People who eat plenty of polyphenol-rich foods can cut their risk of type 2 diabetes in half and improve their heart health.

Polyphenols suppress enzymes that activate inflammation. They are also antioxidants, further helping to quell inflammation. Cloves have about 15 percent polyphenols by weight, whereas veggies may have only 0.1 percent. Along with their intrinsic anti-inflammatory effects, polyphenols act as prebiotic fiber, feeding your beneficial gut microbes.

The researchers saw an increase in microbes that produce butyrate, an amazing substance that nourishes and heals the cells lining your gut, ensuring that they keep toxins and pathogens out of your bloodstream. Butyrate does more: It can travel to the brain, encouraging the growth of new nerve cells, improving cognition and memory.

The gut-brain axis responds to inflammation by putting you into sickness mode, where you just want to be left alone to lie in bed while your joy evaporates. This behavior is pretty much identical to depression. Depression and anxiety overlap strongly with gut issues, and psychiatrists are increasingly turning to prebiotics and probiotics to augment other treatments. Microbes that can improve your mood are called psychobiotics. While polyphenols boost good bacteria, they also fight harmful bacteria like C. difficile, Salmonella, and E. coli.

It's not exactly news that spices are healthy. Some 2,500 years ago, Hippocrates believed that spices like cinnamon, coriander, and saffron had healing powers. Nutmeg was prescribed for flatulence, and enticingly, ginger was used as an aphrodisiac. The Kama Sutra recommends cardamom, ginger, nutmeg, and cloves for a good time. Spices were, and still are, sexy.

Spices were also expensive. In 410 AD, the Visigoths sacked Rome and held it hostage for a ransom of 5,000 pounds of gold and 3,000 pounds of pepper. At the time, spices were as good as gold. In those days, traders conspired to restrict the supply of spices, ensuring high prices. Their trade routes were kept secret, and excess crops were burned. It was a cutthroat business, but smuggled seeds soon cropped up in tropical farms around the world. Today, supply chains are efficient, and spices are far cheaper, so there’s little to stop you from trying some.

Don't be afraid to play with combinations of spices to create your own custom spice blend. Use it as a rub on fish or meat before cooking or sprinkle some spice on food after it’s cooked. Pro tip: Buy spices whole and grind them just before using to get the maximum joy. Spices have a lot of volatile oils that evaporate over time and deplete the magnificent aromas.

If you have GERD or IBS, start slowly. Spices activate microbes, which can create gas if you overdo it. But over time, your microbes will adjust.

For everyone else, spices are awesome. They may not help you control the universe, but they taste great, and they can improve your mood. Once you get started, you’ll realize that life is too short to be stuck with bland food. Kick it up a notch.

Petersen, Kristina S., Samantha Anderson, Jeremy R. Chen See, Jillian Leister, Penny M. Kris-Etherton, and Regina Lamendella. “Herbs and Spices Modulate Gut Bacterial Composition in Adults at Risk for CVD: Results of a Prespecified Exploratory Analysis from a Randomized, Crossover, Controlled-Feeding Study.” The Journal of Nutrition 152, no. 11 (2022): 2461–70. https://doi.org/10.1093/jn/nxac201.

Khine, Wei Wei Thwe, Sumanto Haldar, Shou De Loi, and Yuan-Kun Lee. “A Single Serving of Mixed Spices Alters Gut Microflora Composition: A Dose–Response Randomised Trial.” Scientific Reports 11, no. 1 (2021): 11264. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-021-90453-7.

Diacova, Tatiana, David Heber, and Zhaoping Li. “Spices Beyond Antioxidants: From the Gut to the Brain.” Nutrition Reviews 84, no. Suppl 1 (2026): 52–69. https://doi.org/10.1093/nutrit/nuaf176.

Lu, Qing-Yi, Anna M. Rasmussen, Jieping Yang, et al. “Mixed Spices at Culinary Doses Have Prebiotic Effects in Healthy Adults: A Pilot Study.” Nutrients 11, no. 6 (2019): 1425. https://doi.org/10.3390/nu11061425.

#4904. 'The Biggest Opportunity in Crypto Is Already Underway': Solana Bets Big on AI Agents

'The Biggest Opportunity in Crypto Is Already Underway': Solana Bets Big on AI Agents

AI agents are becoming the internet's main users, and the Solana Foundation wants its blockchain to be how they pay for things.

"The biggest opportunity in crypto is already underway," Rishin Sharma, who leads AI at the Solana Foundation, said at the Breakpoint conference in London.

Sharma used his presentation to showcase Solana.ai, a new homepage for the network's AI ecosystem, which the foundation launched last week. More than 120 teams are now building AI products on Solana across payments, trading and infrastructure, he said.

Bots and other automated software accounted for more than 53% of global web traffic in 2025, up from 51% the year before, according to cybersecurity firm Imperva's latest Bad Bot Report, which Sharma cited. Agents have also come to dominate AI usage: API keys classified as agentic accounted for 76% of the tokens processed through model marketplace OpenRouter in October 2026, up from 8% a year earlier, according to the company's monthly data recap.

At the same time, AI is getting much cheaper. Sharma's slides showed the price of a million tokens falling from $37.50 for GPT-4 to $0.14 for GPT-5 nano, a 270-fold drop, while monthly token use has risen around 330-fold.

"Which model is smarter matters a little bit less, and what matters more is what the model can actually do," Sharma said. "What are the tools it's using? What are the data it's able to access? And what money can it functionally use?"

Payments are where that shift is showing up first, Sharma said. The x402 standard, which lets AI agents pay for online services, has processed more than 400 million payments and $100 million in volume, up from zero a year ago, he said. Solana-based BlockRun, which he described as the largest x402 server, has handled more than 60 million transactions in the past two months.

The foundation has also launched payment channels that it says can handle 1 million payments per second, a private AI service offering attested private inference on Solana, and Pay.sh, a server integrating more than 100 APIs that agents can pay for, Sharma said. He added that the foundation is working with companies including Google, Alibaba and ByteDance to let them charge for their AI models through agent payments.

Next, the foundation plans to publish benchmarks for how well AI models perform financial tasks, and to launch an on-ramp that lets users convert cash to stablecoins and hand them to their AI assistants, he said.

Macro investor and Real Vision founder Raoul Pal, speaking earlier at the conference, argued that agents are not yet real economic actors. "They're like kids," he said. "These are young kids who spend nothing on a few sweets and stuff." But as stocks and other assets move onchain, he said, agents will start using blockchains to trade and settle, and "the value accrues to the underlying layer one."

Solana has been building toward this for months. The foundation teamed up with Google Cloud on a stablecoin payments service for AI agents in May, while the x402 Foundation, backed by Coinbase and the Linux Foundation, is developing the payments standard. Franklin Templeton has described agentic AI as a killer use case for crypto.

#4901. Nvidia shares fall 4% as board reportedly rules out Huang return

The chipmaker’s board is reportedly focused on investigating Huang’s conduct, even as a petition calling for his reinstatement gathers more than 187,000 signatures.

Nvidia’s board is reportedly not considering reinstating co-founder Jensen Huang as CEO, despite a growing campaign for his return and public support from US President Donald Trump, as the chipmaker’s shares fell 4.1% on Monday.

The decline came as Bitcoin and US equities faced pressure ahead of Nvidia’s quarterly earnings and following Anthropic’s IPO announcement, adding to nerves around the artificial intelligence sector.

A person familiar with the matter told CNBC that Nvidia’s board was not discussing Huang’s return and remained focused on investigating his conduct. ABC News separately confirmed with a source familiar with the matter that the board was not considering reinstating him as CEO.

Huang resigned last week following reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with an alleged Chinese spy, with chief financial officer Colette Kress taking over as interim CEO. Nvidia’s shares have fallen 27% from a month ago.

The report comes despite growing support for his return. A public petition set up last week called “Stand with Jensen” has attracted more than 187,000 signatures, according to its organizers, who list Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney among its supporters.

US President Donald Trump also weighed in, saying Monday he would not mind seeing Jensen Huang return as chief executive officer of Nvidia.

“I would certainly not mind seeing that,” he told reporters as he departed the White House for a trip to China. “He could return. There’s a big group of people that would like him to return.”

#4900. Morning Minute: The CFTC Officially Greenlights Perps in the US, Starting with Backpack

Morning Minute: The CFTC Officially Greenlights Perps in the US, Starting with Backpack

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🏛️ Backpack and tZERO Win the First Onchain Derivatives Approvals

For years, perps were the product American traders couldn’t legally touch. On Monday, that officially changed.

Chairman Michael Selig approved applications from Backpack and tZERO to run regulated derivatives exchanges and clearinghouses that execute and settle on blockchain rails. Selig called it “an important milestone in the evolution of American financial markets.”

Backpack got both pieces at once, a Designated Contract Market designation to run the exchange and a Derivatives Clearing Organization registration to clear its own trades. It plans to offer Americans round-the-clock leveraged crypto perps on a single global order book, accepting both fiat and crypto as collateral with cross-margin across positions. tZERO received its exchange approval a week earlier, after already holding clearing registration, and is building it into a derivatives vertical inside its infrastructure business.

Backpack US president Mark Wetjen, himself a former CFTC commissioner, framed it as “moving price discovery from offshore unregulated venues to the United States.” CEO Armani Ferrante said the firm enters markets “fully regulated from day one.” Backpack already runs licensed platforms in Europe, Asia and the UAE.

The CFTC has certainly been busy this month. They cleared single-stock perpetual futures on November 3. It issued guidance on November 5 telling federally regulated exchanges they generally cannot restrict access by state. Now it has approved blockchain-native exchanges and clearinghouses.

Selig is building an onshore home for every product the offshore venues were built to serve, and he is doing it through staff action rather than legislation, with one sitting commissioner and nomination hearings for two more this week.

Hyperliquid is the obvious candidate and also the one that has publicly said it doesn’t want this. Jeff Yan spent his Bloomberg interview this month arguing Hyperliquid is infrastructure rather than an exchange, and that regulators should oversee the firms deploying markets on top rather than the chain underneath. His US route is HIP-3 partners taking on the compliance burden, with ICE investing in Trade.xyz and Kraken’s parent reportedly in talks. Backpack getting a DCM and a DCO makes that argument harder, because it demonstrates a crypto-native venue can simply register.

Then there’s Lighter. It has run Robinhood Wallet’s perps since July on a dedicated instance with a 50/50 revenue split, which is the partner model rather than the registration model. The registered model opens up with a CFTC license, and many think Lighter could very well be next.

One thing’s certain—the CFTC is serious about bringing perps onshore. From Selig himself, “I look forward to building on that milestone.” And we will find out soon enough who’s next…

#4897. What If 'I Know' Is Our Biggest Limitation?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

Mentally and physically, I was about as flexible as a broomstick. But I had promised myself that when I arrived in Seattle, I would try things that would get me out of my comfort zone—like yoga.

I was a physician with fairly rigid ideas about what made sense and what didn’t fit my worldview. I was also in the habit of always being two steps ahead, planning what came next. Yoga had other ideas. Standing on one leg left no room to plan whether the union of body, mind, and spirit made sense. All I could do was stay in the present moment and focus on one simple task: keeping my body from falling over.

Had I insisted on what I thought I already knew, I could easily have decided yoga wasn’t for me. Instead, I got curious. That curiosity led me to discover the enormous benefits of meditation, helped me make new friendships, deepened my spiritual connection, inspired me to change my career, and led me to meet my wife Danielle at a yoga retreat. I couldn’t have predicted any of it. Looking back, I simply let myself wonder and wander into the unknown.

We begin life curious. Babies reach for things just beyond their grasp, and children ask endless questions about how things work and why people behave the way they do. But as we grow older, we accumulate knowledge, experiences, and beliefs that help us navigate the world. And the more familiar things become, the less likely we are to question them. We start confusing what we know with what we believe, and what we believe with what is actually true.

We form fixed ideas about other people, about how life works, and especially about ourselves. Without realizing it, we turn our assumptions into invisible boundaries. Eventually, our need for certainty can become a surprisingly restrictive force in our lives.

We can travel to 50 countries, try 20 different hobbies, and still be rigid in our thinking. How often do you dismiss something before you give yourself a chance to explore it? You might avoid a new hobby because you’re convinced you won’t be good at it. You decide someone doesn’t like you based on how they looked at you or something they said. Or you accept your inner critic’s verdict that you are not good enough, that you cannot change, or that certain things aren’t possible for you. But are these conclusions facts, or are they interpretations you have never questioned? Genuine curiosity means being willing to question what we believe we already know, particularly about other people.

I recently heard a wonderful story about a woman called Grace, in her late 80s, who, much to her children’s surprise, decided to volunteer at a homeless shelter. She had held rather rigid opinions about people whose backgrounds and circumstances were very different from her own, and everyone expected her to give up after a few days.

Instead, Grace found her role. When new residents arrive, she guides them around, helping them pick up sheets, towels, and food, and walks with them for about an hour. Along the way, they tell her all about their lives. Then she says, “Honey, I love you,” and gives them a hug.

Today, the residents love her. And they are all coming to her birthday party.

Grace had walked in seeing people through the lens of stereotypes. By listening, she discovered their shared humanity and qualities that inspired her: resilience, tenacity, and kindness, even from people who had experienced very little kindness themselves. She told her daughter, “I thought I had something to offer the residents, but it turns out what they give me is so much more profound.”

But to become genuinely curious, we also need the humility to recognize that there is more we don't know than we know. The moment we accept "I know" as the final answer, we close ourselves off to other possibilities. Intellectual humility means being willing to question our own beliefs, acknowledge that we might be wrong, and consider perspectives that differ from our own (Porter et al., 2022).

This openness can also transform how we relate to others. Studies have found that curious people tend to respond less aggressively, even when provoked, and that asking follow-up questions and showing genuine interest makes us more likable to others (Kashdan et al., 2013; Huang et al., 2017). Similarly, a meta-analysis of 515 studies found that getting to know people from different social groups can help reduce prejudice and challenge stereotypes (Pettigrew & Tropp, 2006).

Grace's experience at the shelter illustrates this beautifully. By spending time with people she might otherwise have judged from a distance, listening to their stories, and becoming genuinely interested in their lives, she discovered not only unexpected friendships but also qualities she deeply admired.

The next time you catch yourself judging others or yourself, feeling defensive, or becoming convinced that something cannot change, pause and ask:

Perhaps the greatest limitation in our lives isn’t what we don’t know, but what we believe we already know. Curiosity asks us to make peace with how much we don’t know, to let go of the need to have all the answers, and to be willing to say, “I might be wrong” or, simply, “I wonder what would happen if…”

That willingness took me from a yoga mat to a life I never could have planned.

Huang, K., Yeomans, M., Brooks, A. W., Minson, J., & Gino, F. (2017). It doesn’t hurt to ask: Question-asking increases liking. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 113(3), 430–452. https://doi.org/10.1037/pspi0000097

Kashdan, T. B., DeWall, C. N., Pond, R. S., Silvia, P. J., Lambert, N. M., Fincham, F. D., & Keller, P. S. (2013). Curiosity protects against interpersonal aggression: Cross-sectional, daily process, and behavioral evidence. Journal of Personality, 81(1), 87–102. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-6494.2012.00783.x

Pettigrew, T. F., & Tropp, L. R. (2006). A meta-analytic test of intergroup contact theory. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 90(5), 751–783. https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.90.5.751

Porter, T., Elnakouri, A., Meyers, E. A., Shibayama, T., Jayawickreme, E., & Grossmann, I. (2022). Predictors and consequences of intellectual humility. Nature Reviews Psychology, 1(9), 524–536. https://doi.org/10.1038/s44159-022-00081-9

Schutte, N. S., & Malouff, J. M. (2023). A meta-analytic investigation of the impact of curiosity-enhancing interventions. Current Psychology, 42, 20374–20384. https://doi.org/10.1007/s12144-022-03107-w

#4896. Hackers inject credit card, crypto-stealing malware into 178 major websites

Ledger and Coinpass were among the businesses whose websites served malware designed to steal visitors’ crypto and credit card details, according to security researchers.

Update (Nov. 17 at 4:43 am UTC): This article has been updated to include a statement from Salesforce and analysis of the stolen funds by Galaxy Digital’s Alex Thorn.

Major global brands including Ledger, Salesforce and BBC served cryptocurrency and credit card-stealing malware to website visitors on Monday, according to security researchers, the latest in a wave of wide-reaching cyberattacks.

Dubbed “Optimjack” by researcher Amirul Iman, the campaign delivered malicious code through website scripts, including those hosted by Optimizely, a website optimization platform. The malware can replace legitimate cryptocurrency wallet addresses with attacker-controlled alternatives when visitors copy addresses. It also captured credit card details entered into website forms, according to a separate analysis by cybersecurity firm StepSecurity.

The campaign affected website scripts belonging to 178 organizations, including hardware wallet Ledger, OANDA / Coinpass and trading platforms IG, Interactive Investor and TMS Brokers, according to Report URI’s investigation.

Other affected brands included IKEA, Salesforce, Zoom, the BBC and CNN — websites that collectively attract millions of visitors a day.

A spokesperson for Optimizely confirmed to Cointelegraph on Monday that an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability in its Experimentation account signup flow to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects.

“We have blocked access to the vulnerable path and deployed a fix,” they said. “We have removed the injected code from affected projects. We have also ended active sessions, revoked tokens, and reset access for affected user accounts. Finally, we have blocked any code from being delivered in Cloudflare,” they said, adding that they’re continuing to investigate and have notified all affected customers.

BBC was among the first websites seen serving the Optimizely injection at 4:21 am UTC on Monday, according to Report URI CEO Scott Helme. By 9:52 pm UTC, only one of the 188 infected snippets still contained malicious code, according to Report URI, with Ledger’s snippet cleaned at about 8:41 pm UTC.

Cointelegraph reached out to Ledger, Coinpass, and a number of other major businesses reported as impacted by the malicious injection.

A spokesperson for Salesforce told Cointelegraph it was investigating unauthorized modifications to Optimizely scripts used on certain Salesforce websites.

“As a precaution, Salesforce is confirming Optimizely scripts are disabled across preview and production instances.”

Optimizely lets businesses run experiments and custom code on their websites through JavaScript snippets. Attackers inserted malicious code into those customer projects, causing otherwise legitimate scripts to load the malware when visitors opened affected pages.

Once loaded, the malware could replace legitimate cryptocurrency wallet addresses with attacker-controlled alternatives, including addresses copied to a visitor’s clipboard. A user could then unknowingly paste the attacker’s address when making a transfer.

Related: PHP flaw draws millions of exploit attempts, Cloudflare CEO warns

The script also watched for credit card numbers, expiry dates and security codes entered into website forms and sent captured details to an attacker-controlled server. However, it could not read payment fields isolated inside third-party payment providers’ cross-origin frames, StepSecurity said.

Visitors using iPhones and iPads also faced an additional threat: the malicious script loaded code linked to the Coruna and DarkSword iOS exploit kits, according to Helme.

Researchers have yet to establish how attackers obtained access to modify the Optimizely projects.

Despite the widespread attack, crypto addresses identified as linked to the malware received only around $7,100 in cryptocurrency on Monday, according to blockchain records checked by Cointelegraph.

StepSecurity’s separate analysis put funds received or held across the wallets at roughly $6,600, noting that some addresses had activity predating the attack.

Alex Thorn, head of firmwide research at Galaxy Digital, estimated that victims have only sent around $2,300 in total to the addresses. In an analysis posted on X, Thorn said most of the Bitcoin receipts appeared to be the attacker’s own funds routed through an exchange back to their wallet, rather than proceeds from victims.

The campaign follows a string of recent threats to website security. In October, government agencies and other organizations took websites offline as a precaution over critical PHP vulnerabilities that could allow attackers to take control of servers. Polymarket also temporarily suspended trading on Oct. 29 after a third-party provider was compromised, amid reports of users’ accounts being drained.

#4894. Standard Life Chair Wants Tokenized Gilts to Open UK Government Bonds to Retail Savers

Standard Life Chair Wants Tokenized Gilts to Open UK Government Bonds to Retail Savers

Tokenization could help ordinary British savers buy government bonds more easily, and should not become a tool only for big financial institutions, according to Sir Nicholas Lyons, chairman of Standard Life.

"Why should it be so unnecessarily complicated for retail investors to buy gilts that fund the nation's needs?" Lyons said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London.

Lyons, a former Lord Mayor of the City of London whose company has about £480 billion ($633 billion) in assets under administration, said Britain should use tokenization to bring more households into investing. About £850 billion ($1.1 trillion) of household wealth remains in cash, often losing value to inflation, he said.

Tokenization could cut administrative costs, make transfers simpler and let savers invest in small amounts in products that would otherwise be uneconomic, Lyons said. More retail ownership of gilts would make the UK less reliant on foreign buyers and could help erode the risk premium the country has paid on its debt since the September 2022 mini-budget, he added.

The remarks come as the UK prepares to issue its first digitally native government bond, known as DIGIT. Earlier this month, the Treasury confirmed roles for six banks supporting the pilot, with issuance expected in the first quarter of 2027, Lyons said.

"Government bonds sit at the heart of our financial system," he said, noting that they underpin liquidity, provide collateral and set reference rates for other assets. Showing that tokenized gilts can be issued, settled and used as collateral efficiently would lay "an important foundation for much broader digital capital markets," he said.

Lyons credited the Financial Conduct Authority, the Bank of England and the Treasury for progress so far, including the Digital Securities Sandbox and work to let tokenized securities be used as collateral.

The Bank of England has also turned its attention to stablecoins: an executive has said it is treating them as a new form of money, it eased its planned stablecoin rules in June, and it has since been handed a legal duty to foster stablecoin innovation. Lyons cited a July report by Chris Woolard, the government's wholesale digital markets champion, which estimated that faster adoption of digital markets could add as much as £33 billion ($43.5 billion) a year to UK economic output by 2035.

But the competition is moving fast, he warned, pointing to the SEC's innovation exemption for tokenized stock trading in the U.S., and initiatives in Europe, Singapore, Hong Kong, Switzerland and the Middle East.

Lyons set out four priorities: making the digital gilt pilot a success, accelerating tokenized collateral and repo markets, promoting interoperability to avoid "a collection of competing digital platforms that recreate the fragmentation of the existing system," and ensuring retail investors share in the benefits.

He also acknowledged the risks, citing concerns raised by the Financial Times' Gillian Tett that AI-accelerated cyber threats and faster-moving money could fuel a run on deposits. "Tokenization does not automatically make an investment safer, more liquid, or more stable, or suitable for retail investors," he said. "Fractional ownership of a risky asset remains ownership of a risky asset."

"We're not pursuing tokenization for the sake of technological leadership," Lyons said. "We're pursuing better capital markets, more productive investment, greater economic prosperity, and wider participation in the creation of wealth and jobs."

#4891. Trezor customer data exposed in 3rd partner breach this year

Trezor warned an attacker has been able to access customer names, phone numbers and delivery addresses linked to 27,000 orders from one of its shipping providers.

Hardware wallet maker Trezor has disclosed its third partner data breach this year, warning that an attacker accessed personal information linked to approximately 27,000 orders held by shipping provider Skladon.

The exposed information includes customers’ names, phone numbers, delivery addresses, email addresses and purchase details for orders placed between Aug. 9 and Nov. 7.

Trezor said Monday that it believes the attacker exported the data. Its own systems were not compromised, and customers’ devices remain secure, the company said.

The disclosure follows an August breach at shipping provider ShipMonk that initially affected about 13,700 customers. Trezor later said another approximately 67,000 US customers were affected, including people whose records ShipMonk had assured it were deleted.

In September, a separate breach at marketing platform Brevo exposed 347,149 email addresses from Trezor’s newsletter list.

“Treat any unexpected call, text, email or letter about your order as suspicious, and be alert to potential risks to your physical security,” the company said on X. “Trezor will never cold-call you or ask for your wallet backup.”

The disclosure came as Trezor launched its anonymous pickup service in 18 European countries, allowing customers to order devices without giving out home addresses.

“For crypto holders, a leaked shipping record does more harm than most. It links a person, a home address and the fact that they own crypto,” it said.
